• Begin reintroduction phase of elimination diet 
Month 5-6: Optimize Nutrition 
• Anti-inflammatory diet as foundation 
• Omega-3s: 2-3g daily 
• Vitamin D: optimize to 60-80 ng/mL 
• Magnesium, zinc, selenium as needed 
• Continue gut healing protocol 
Month 7-12: Integrate and Monitor 
• Continue anti-inflammatory eating with 80/20 flexibility 
• Ongoing probiotic rotation 
• Stress management and nervous system work 
• Trauma processing if needed 
• Retest inflammatory markers and antibodies every 3-4 months 
Protocol Shift:  
Autoimmune disease is not "bad luck" you must passively accept. The immune system 
is dysregulated due to modifiable factors: gut health, food sensitivities, toxins, stress, 
nutrient deficiencies. Address the root causes while working with medical treatment. 
Your body can heal. 

===== PDF PAGE 155 =====
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
PART IV: THE RESISTANCE 
Navigating a System That Won't Change Fast Enough 

===== PDF PAGE 156 =====
138 | P a g e  
 
 
Chapter 12: How to Fire a Bad Doctor 
Emma Rodriguez was a medical journalist. She'd spent a decade writing about 
healthcare policy, patient advocacy, and medical innovation. She knew how the system 
worked. She knew her rights. 
And still, she couldn't get anyone to take her pain seriously. 
"I had endometriosis for seven years before I got a diagnosis," she told me over video 
call from her home office in Boston. "Seven years of debilitating pain, heavy periods 
that left me anemic, pain during sex, chronic fatigue. And doctor after doctor told me 
it was normal. That periods are supposed to hurt." 
The pain got so bad she was missing work. She went to the ER three times, convinced 
something was seriously wrong. 
"They did ultrasounds, bloodwork, pelvic exams. Everything looked 'normal.' They 
prescribed ibuprofen and sent me home. One ER doctor suggested I was drug-seeking. 
I was a medical journalist in excruciating pain, and he thought I was lying to get 
painkillers." 
At 32, Emma finally found a gynecologist who listened who did a diagnostic 
laparoscopy and found stage 3 endometriosis throughout her pelvis. 
"The surgeon said, 'This is extensive. You've had this for years. Why didn't anyone do 
this surgery sooner?'" 
Emma knew why. Because for seven years, doctors had dismissed her pain as normal, 
psychological, or exaggerated. 
"I'm a journalist. I'm educated. I'm articulate. I have good insurance. And it still took 
seven years and eleven different doctors to get diagnosed." 
After her surgery, Emma did something radical. She recorded her follow-up 
appointment with the OBGYN who had missed her endometriosis for three years. 
 
The Appointment Recording That Changed Hospital Policy 

===== PDF PAGE 157 =====
139 | P a g e  
 
 
In most states, you can legally record conversations you're part of without the other 
person's consent (called "one-party consent"). Emma looked up her state law: 
Massachusetts allowed it. 
At her follow-up appointment, she placed her phone on the exam table, recording app 
open. 
"I told my doctor I'd had surgery and been diagnosed with stage 3 endometriosis," 
Emma said. "And I asked her: 'Why didn't you suggest a laparoscopy when I came to 
you for three years complaining of severe pelvic pain?'" 
The doctor's response, captured on recording: "Well, endometriosis is hard to 
diagnose. Ultrasounds don't always show it. And you never said your pain was that 
severe." 
Emma had her records with her. She'd brought copies. 
"I showed her my own chart notes, where she'd documented 'patient reports 8/10 
pelvic pain, dysmenorrhea, dyspareunia.' I'd reported severe pain. She'd written it 
down. And still done nothing." 
The doctor became defensive. "We can't do surgery on every woman who has painful 
periods. That would be excessive." 
Emma stopped the recording. She transcribed it that night. And she filed a formal 
complaint with the hospital's patient advocacy office, including: 
• The recording transcript 
• Her medical records showing documented severe pain 
• The surgical report showing extensive endometriosis 
• Research showing that average time to endometriosis diagnosis is 7.5 years 
due to medical dismissal 
The hospital's response surprised her. They didn't dismiss her complaint. They 
investigated. 

===== PDF PAGE 158 =====
140 | P a g e  
 
 
"Three months later, I got a letter. The hospital had implemented new guidelines for 
evaluating pelvic pain. They'd added endometriosis screening questions to their 
intake forms. They'd created a protocol: if a patient reports severe dysmenorrhea for 
more than 6 months, refer to a specialist." 
One complaint. One recording. One woman refusing to be silent. 
"It won't help me," Emma said. "I already suffered for seven years. But maybe the 
women who come after me won't have to." 
 
Red Flags of Medical Gaslighting 
Through her journalism and her own experience, Emma identified the patterns. These 
are the red flags that a doctor is not taking you seriously: 
1. "It's all in your head" Or variations: "It's just stress," "You're anxious," "It's 
psychosomatic." 
Why this is gaslighting: Pain, fatigue, and physical symptoms have physiological 
causes. Even if stress or anxiety are contributing factors, dismissing physical 
symptoms as purely psychological prevents proper investigation. 
2. "Your labs are normal" When you're told everything is fine despite persistent 
symptoms. 
Why this is gaslighting: "Normal" lab ranges are often based on sick populations or 
male norms. Also, many conditions (like perimenopause, early autoimmune disease, 
insulin resistance) show up symptomatically before labs become abnormal. 
3. "That's just part of being a woman" About periods, hormonal changes, pain, 
fatigue. 
Why this is gaslighting: While some discomfort is normal, severe or debilitating 
symptoms are not. This dismissal prevents diagnosis of conditions like endometriosis, 
PCOS, fibroids, adenomyosis. 

===== PDF PAGE 159 =====
141 | P a g e  
 
 
4. "You're too young for that" About perimenopause, hormonal issues, chronic 
conditions. 
Why this is gaslighting: Many conditions can start earlier than "typical." 
Perimenopause can begin in mid-30s. Autoimmune disease often emerges in 20s-30s. 
Age-based dismissal delays diagnosis. 
5. "Lose weight and you'll feel better" For every symptom, regardless of actual 
weight or health status. 
Why this is gaslighting: While weight can be a factor in some conditions, using it as 
a blanket explanation prevents investigation of actual causes. Also, many conditions 
cause weight gain rather than the reverse. 
6. "I don't believe in that" About emerging research, functional medicine 
approaches, patient-reported concerns. 
Why this is gaslighting: Medicine evolves. Dismissing all approaches outside 
conventional practice limits patient options and disregards valid research. 
7. "You're too focused on this" Or: "You're overthinking it," "You're being a 
hypochondriac," "Stop Googling." 
Why this is gaslighting: Patients who research their symptoms and advocate for 
themselves are being responsible, not neurotic. This response shames women for self-
education. 
8. Visible dismissiveness Rolling eyes, checking phone, interrupting, not making eye 
contact, rushing through appointments. 
Why this is gaslighting: Non-verbal communication that conveys you're wasting 
their time or your concerns aren't valid. 
The Bureau's Doctor Dossier: How to Prepare for Appointments 
Emma developed a system she now teaches to other women: the Doctor Dossier. 
What to bring to every appointment: 
1. Symptom Log (from Chapter 6) 

===== PDF PAGE 160 =====
142 | P a g e  
 
 
• Daily tracking of symptoms for at least 30 days 
• Visual charts showing patterns 
• Highlight correlations (cycle phase, food, stress, etc.) 
2. Medical History Summary (1 page) 
• Current symptoms with timeline 
• Past diagnoses 
• Medications and supplements 
• Allergies 
• Surgical history 
• Family history 
3. List of Questions (prioritized) Write them down beforehand. Don't rely on 
memory. 
• Most important questions first (in case appointment is cut short) 
• Specific, clear questions 
• Example: "Can we test my thyroid antibodies, not just TSH?" 
4. Lab Results 
• Bring copies of all recent labs 
• Highlight abnormal or borderline results 
• Note symptoms that correlate with results 
5. Research (if applicable) 
• 1-2 peer-reviewed studies relevant to your concerns 
• Highlighted key findings 
• "I found this research and wondered if it applies to my situation" 
6. Recording Device (if legal in your state) 

===== PDF PAGE 161 =====
143 | P a g e  
 
 
• Check your state's recording laws 
• If one-party consent: simply record 
• If two-party consent: ask permission ("Do you mind if I record so I can 
remember your recommendations?") 
7. Advocate 
• Bring a friend or family member if possible 
• They can take notes, remember details, and witness dismissive behavior 
• Studies show doctors take patients more seriously when someone else is 
present 
 
The approach: 
Start with the summary: "I've been experiencing [symptoms] for [duration]. I've 
tracked them for 30 days and noticed [pattern]. I've brought my data to show you." 
Present evidence: Hand them your visual charts and symptom log. 
Ask specific questions: "Based on these symptoms and patterns, could we test for 
[condition]?" or "What do you think could be causing this?" 
Document their response: Take notes or record. If they dismiss you, you have 
evidence. 
Request next steps: "If we don't find anything with these tests, what's the next step?" 
Don't leave without a plan. 
If dismissed: "I hear that you think this is [stress/normal/age]. But I'm still 
experiencing significant symptoms that are affecting my quality of life. What can we 
do to investigate further?" 
 
 
 

===== PDF PAGE 162 =====
144 | P a g e  
 
 
When to Get a Second (or Fifth) Opinion 
Emma's rule: If you're not satisfied with the answer, get another opinion. 
Situations that warrant a second opinion: 
1. Diagnosis seems wrong Your symptoms don't match, or your intuition says 
something's off. 
2. Doctor dismisses your concerns without investigation Tells you it's 
stress/normal/age without doing tests or exams. 
3. Treatment isn't working You've followed the protocol for appropriate time and 
nothing has improved. 
4. Doctor can't explain what's wrong "Everything looks normal" but you feel 
terrible. 
5. Major treatment decision Surgery, starting/stopping hormones, long-term 
medication always get a second opinion. 
6. You don't feel heard or respected Trust is essential in healthcare. If it's not there, 
find someone else. 
 
How to find a better doctor: 
1. Ask for referrals from: 
• Other patients with similar conditions (online support groups are invaluable) 
• Other healthcare providers you trust 
• Local functional medicine or integrative health centers 
2. Research providers online: 
• Check reviews (especially looking for mentions of "listens," "thorough," "takes 
time") 
• Look for specializations relevant to your condition 

===== PDF PAGE 163 =====
145 | P a g e  
 
 
• Check their training and certifications 
3. Interview them: Most doctors offer brief phone consultations or will answer basic 
questions before you book. 
Ask: 
• "How do you approach [your condition]?" 
• "Do you work with patients on [diet/lifestyle/supplements]?" 
• "How much time do you typically spend with patients?" 
• "What's your approach when labs are normal but symptoms persist?" 
4. Consider functional or integrative medicine: These practitioners often spend 
more time with patients and address root causes. 
5. Be willing to travel or do telehealth: If there's no one locally who specializes in 
your condition, telehealth has expanded access dramatically. 
Telemedicine and Women-Focused Care Alternatives 
"After my endometriosis experience, I stopped going to conventional doctors for 
hormonal issues," Emma said. "I use a combination of telemedicine and functional 
medicine now." 
Women-focused telehealth platforms: 
For Hormones: 
• Paloma Health (thyroid specialists) 
• Midi Health (perimenopause/menopause) 
• Alloy (menopause) 
• Evernow (hormones) 
For General Women's Health: 
• Maven Clinic (comprehensive women's and family health) 
• Tia (women's health, some locations have physical clinics) 

===== PDF PAGE 164 =====
146 | P a g e  
 
 
• Parsley Health (functional medicine, comprehensive) 
For Mental Health: 
• Cerebral (psychiatry, therapy, focuses on women's hormonal mental health) 
• Minded (psychiatry for women) 
Benefits of these platforms: 
• Providers specialized in women's health 
• Often better at recognizing hormonal issues 
• More time per appointment (30-45 minutes vs. 10-15) 
• Easier to get comprehensive testing 
• Telemedicine = accessible from anywhere 
Limitations: 
• May not take insurance (though many offer insurance billing) 
• Can't do procedures or physical exams 
• May not be appropriate for acute/emergency issues 
"I use telemedicine for hormones and functional issues," Emma said. "And I keep a 
conventional doctor for acute stuff infections, injuries, urgent care. It's a hybrid model, 
but it works." 
 
The Bureau Resource: The Patient Advocacy Script Library 
The Bureau compiled scripts for common scenarios where women need to advocate 
for themselves: 
Script 1: When a doctor says "your labs are normal" 
"I understand my labs fall within the reference range. However, I'm still experiencing 
significant symptoms [list top 3]. Can we discuss: 

===== PDF PAGE 165 =====
147 | P a g e  
 
 
1. Whether my results are optimal versus just normal 
2. Additional tests that might reveal what's causing my symptoms 
3. A referral to a specialist who might have more expertise with these symptoms" 
Script 2: When a doctor dismisses symptoms as stress/anxiety 
"I hear that stress might be a contributing factor. However, these symptoms are 
significantly impacting my daily life and have persisted despite [stress management 
efforts I've made]. I'd like to rule out physiological causes before attributing this to 
stress. Can we run [specific tests] to make sure we're not missing something?" 
Script 3: When a doctor won't order tests you're requesting 
"I understand you don't think these tests are necessary. Can you explain your 
reasoning? [Listen to their answer.] I appreciate that perspective. However, given my 
symptoms and family history/research I've found, I'd like to have these tests for my 
own peace of mind. If you're not comfortable ordering them, can you refer me to 
someone who might be?" 
Script 4: When a doctor says "you're too young" for perimenopause/hormones  
"I understand that average age for [condition] is [age]. However, I've been tracking my 
symptoms for [duration] and they align with [condition]. Can we test my hormone 
levels to rule it in or out, rather than making assumptions based on my age?" 
Script 5: When treatment isn't working 
"I've been on [treatment] for [duration] as you recommended. Unfortunately, I haven't 
seen improvement. What's our next step? Are there other treatment options we 
should consider, or should I see a specialist?" 
Script 6: When you need to fire a doctor 
You don't owe them an explanation, but if you want to provide one: 
"I appreciate the care you've provided, but I don't feel we're the right fit. I'm going to 
seek care elsewhere. I'd like copies of my complete medical records sent to [new 
provider] or to me directly. What's the process for that?" 

===== PDF PAGE 166 =====
148 | P a g e  
 
 
Or, simply: "I'll be transferring my care to another provider. Please send my records 
to [address/fax]." 
 
Your Rights as a Patient 
Emma's journalism uncovered information many women don't know: 
You have the right to: 
1. Your complete medical records Request them at any time. Providers must comply 
within 30 days (HIPAA law). 
2. Refuse treatment You can say no to any test, procedure, or medication. Informed 
consent means you decide. 
3. A second opinion Always. No doctor can prevent you from seeking another 
opinion. 
4. Ask questions About diagnosis, treatment options, alternatives, risks, benefits. If 
your doctor won't answer, that's a red flag. 
5. File complaints 
• With the medical board in your state 
• With the hospital's patient advocacy office 
• With your insurance company 
• With professional organizations 
6. Change providers At any time, for any reason, or no reason at all. 
7. Be treated with respect Free from discrimination, harassment, or dismissive 
behavior. 
8. Have an advocate present Bring whoever you want to appointments (with rare 
exceptions for intimate exams where only medical chaperones may be appropriate). 
 

===== PDF PAGE 167 =====
149 | P a g e  
 
 
What to do if your rights are violated: 
1. Document everything 
• Names, dates, what was said 
• Witnesses if applicable 
• Medical records 
• Recordings if legal 
2. File a formal complaint Start with the practice/hospital patient advocacy office. 
If not resolved, escalate to: 
• State medical board 
• Insurance company 
• Professional associations 
• Civil rights office (if discrimination occurred) 
3. Consider legal consultation Medical malpractice attorneys often offer free 
consultations. Even if you don't pursue legal action, they can advise you on your 
options. 
4. Share your story Online reviews, patient advocacy groups, social media (if 
comfortable). Your story might help other women avoid the same doctor.

What You Need to Know 
You are allowed to fire a doctor who doesn't serve you. 
You are allowed to seek second, third, fourth opinions until you find someone who 
listens. 
You are allowed to bring data, research, and advocates to appointments. 
You are allowed to record appointments (check your state law). 

===== PDF PAGE 169 =====
151 | P a g e  
 
 
You are allowed to file complaints when you're dismissed or mistreated. 
You are allowed to build a healthcare team that works for you, even if it's 
unconventional. 
Medicine is a service you're paying for. If you're not satisfied with the service, find 
a different provider. 
Your time, your money, your body, your choice. 
Don't waste years with doctors who don't believe you. There are providers out there 
who will. Keep looking until you find them. 
And remember Emma's lesson: you are the CEO of your health. Doctors are 
consultants. You make the final decisions. 
Fire the ones who don't serve you. Hire the ones who do. 
You deserve nothing less.

Chapter 13: The Economics of Women's Health 
Dr. Hadiza Bello-Tijani was an economist before she became a women's health 
advocate. 
She'd spent fifteen years analyzing global health systems, conducting cost-benefit 
analyses, and advising governments on healthcare spending. She knew how to put a 
price tag on health outcomes. 
But it wasn't until her own daughter nearly died from undiagnosed PCOS-related 
complications that she turned her economic lens on women's health specifically. 
"My daughter was 22," Hadiza told me from her office at the London School of 
Economics, where she now directs the Women's Health Economics Research Center. 
"She'd been complaining of irregular periods, severe acne, and weight gain for three 
years. Her GP prescribed birth control and told her to diet. No one tested her 
hormones. No one mentioned PCOS." 
At 22, her daughter collapsed at university. She was rushed to the ER with a blood 
sugar of 450. She'd developed type 2 diabetes a known complication of untreated 
PCOS. 
"If someone had diagnosed her PCOS at 19 when symptoms started, we could have 
prevented the diabetes," Hadiza said. "But because it took three years to diagnose, she 
now has a chronic condition she'll manage for life." 
As an economist, Hadiza immediately calculated the cost: medications, monitoring, 
eventual complications, reduced quality of life, lost productivity. 
"The lifetime cost of managing her diabetes will exceed £500,000. The cost of 
diagnosing and treating her PCOS properly would have been perhaps £5,000." 
This 100:1 ratio the cost of neglecting women's health versus the cost of treating it 
properly became Hadiza's obsession. 
 
The $500 Billion Cost of Ignoring Women's Health 

===== PDF PAGE 173 =====
155 | P a g e  
 
 
In 2025, Hadiza led the most comprehensive economic analysis of women's health 
ever conducted, studying healthcare systems in 32 countries. 
The findings were staggering. 
The global economic cost of inadequately addressed women's health issues: 
$500 billion annually. 
This includes: 
Direct medical costs: 
• Treating complications that could have been prevented with earlier diagnosis 
• Emergency care for untreated conditions 
• Unnecessary procedures from misdiagnosis 
• Long-term management of chronic diseases that could have been prevented 
Indirect costs: 
• Lost productivity from untreated symptoms 
• Disability from preventable complications 
• Reduced workforce participation 
• Presenteeism (being at work but impaired by symptoms) 
 
Specific examples from the research: 
Endometriosis: 
• Average diagnostic delay: 7.5 years 
• Average cost during diagnostic period: $22,000 per patient (doctor visits, ER 
visits, imaging, pain management, lost work) 
• Cost of early diagnosis and treatment: $3,000 
• Global cost of diagnostic delay: $78 billion annually 

===== PDF PAGE 174 =====
156 | P a g e  
 
 
PCOS: 
• 70% of cases undiagnosed 
• Lifetime cost of complications (diabetes, cardiovascular disease, infertility): 
$400,000 per patient 
• Cost of early diagnosis and management: $8,000 
• Global cost of underdiagnosis: $142 billion annually 
Perimenopause/Menopause: 
• 80% of women receive no medical support 
• Average productivity loss: $1,800 per woman per year 
• Women leaving workforce due to symptoms: $150 billion in lost earnings 
globally 
• Cost of appropriate hormone therapy and support: $500 per woman per year 
Autoimmune Disease: 
• Average time to diagnosis: 4.5 years 
• Cost of treating late-stage disease: $80,000+ per patient lifetime 
• Cost of early diagnosis and prevention: $12,000 
• Global cost of delayed diagnosis: $94 billion annually 
"This is not just a women's issue," Hadiza emphasized. "This is an economic 
catastrophe. We're spending hundreds of billions treating preventable complications 
while failing to invest thousands in prevention and early treatment." 
Why Women's Health Is Profitable to Ignore 
The most disturbing finding in Hadiza's research was this: the current system is 
economically incentivized to ignore women's health. 
Here's why: 
1. Fee-for-service model rewards treatment, not prevention 

===== PDF PAGE 175 =====
157 | P a g e  
 
 
Doctors and hospitals are paid for procedures, tests, and treatments not for 
preventing disease or spending time with patients. 
Example: 
• 10-minute appointment where doctor dismisses symptoms: $120 
• 45-minute appointment with comprehensive evaluation and prevention plan: 
$250 
• Surgery for advanced endometriosis: $25,000 
The system pays more for the surgery than for prevention. 
2. Diagnostic delay is profitable 
Every year a woman goes undiagnosed: 
• She sees multiple doctors (revenue) 
• Gets multiple tests (revenue) 
• Tries multiple medications (revenue) 
• Maybe visits ER (high revenue) 
Once diagnosed and properly treated, she becomes a lower-revenue patient. 
3. Women's health conditions are chronically underfunded in research 
Less than 1% of healthcare research funding goes to women's health issues (excluding 
breast cancer and reproductive cancers). 
Why? Because: 
• Women's health is seen as "niche" 
• Conditions that primarily affect women are considered less important 
• Drug companies focus on conditions with larger markets (which often means 
conditions that affect men) 
4. Women's health products have higher profit margins than prevention 

===== PDF PAGE 176 =====
158 | P a g e  
 
 
Example: 
• Birth control pills: $20-$50/month, taken for years = ongoing revenue 
• Root cause treatment for PCOS that might resolve need for BC: one-time or 
short-term revenue 
The system prefers chronic management over cures. 
5. Insurance companies save money by denying coverage 
Many insurance plans don't cover: 
• Comprehensive hormone testing 
• Functional medicine approaches 
• Nutrition counseling 
• Pelvic floor PT 
• Long-term therapy 
By denying "non-essential" care, insurance companies reduce short-term costs even 
though this increases long-term costs when preventable conditions worsen. 
"The system isn't broken," Hadiza said. "It's working exactly as designed. The problem 
is it's designed to maximize revenue, not health outcomes." 
 
How to Invest in Your Health Without Going Broke 
The economic reality Hadiza uncovered is frustrating: the healthcare system is 
expensive and often ineffective for women. 
But that doesn't mean women are powerless. It means we need to be strategic about 
how we invest in our health. 
Hadiza's framework: The Health Sovereignty Fund 
The concept: Just as you invest in retirement, you should invest in health. 

===== PDF PAGE 177 =====
159 | P a g e  
 
 
Instead of waiting until you're sick and spending money reactively on treatments and 
medications, invest proactively in prevention and optimization. 
The budget breakdown: 
Tier 1: Essential Investments (Do These First) Monthly cost: $50-150 
• High-quality food (worth paying more for organic, grass-fed, wild-caught) 
• Basic supplements (vitamin D, omega-3, magnesium, probiotic) 
• Stress management tools (therapy, meditation app, gym membership) 
• Sleep optimization (blackout curtains, quality pillow, magnesium) 
Tier 2: Important Investments (Add When Able) Monthly cost: $100-300 
• Comprehensive lab testing (2-4x per year) 
• Functional or integrative medicine consultations (quarterly) 
• Quality protein powder or supplements targeted to your needs 
• Fitness or movement practice you actually enjoy 
• Clean personal care and household products 
Tier 3: Optimal Investments (Luxury But High-Value) Monthly cost: $200-500 
• Pelvic floor PT (if needed) 
• Regular massage or bodywork (nervous system regulation) 
• Continuous glucose monitor (if metabolic issues) 
• Genetic testing to inform prevention 
• Specialty consultations (functional medicine, naturopath) 
 
How to make this affordable: 
1. Reallocate current healthcare spending If you're spending $200/month on 
medications that aren't working, redirect that to root cause investigation. 

===== PDF PAGE 178 =====
160 | P a g e  
 
 
2. Use FSA/HSA accounts Many preventive services and supplements can be 
purchased with pre-tax dollars if deemed medically necessary. 
3. Negotiate with providers Many functional medicine doctors offer package deals 
or payment plans. Ask. 
4. DIY where possible You can order your own labs through services like Ulta Lab 
Tests, WellnessFX, or Paloma Health often cheaper than going through insurance. 
5. Invest strategically based on current needs If you're in crisis, spend more on 
professional support. If you're stable, invest in maintenance and prevention. 
6. Consider it non-negotiable "Women are trained to see healthcare spending on 
ourselves as selfish," Hadiza said. "But investing in your health is investing in your 
capacity to work, care for others, and live fully. It's not selfish. It's essential." 
Insurance Navigation for "Invisible" Conditions 
One of the biggest frustrations women face: insurance often won't cover what they 
actually need. 
 
Hadiza's insurance hacks: 
1. Learn the codes Insurance works on diagnosis codes (ICD-10). If your provider 
codes your visit correctly, more gets covered. 
Example: 
• "Fatigue" alone might not justify hormone testing 
• "Hypothyroidism, unspecified" justifies comprehensive thyroid panel 
• "Irregular menstrual cycle" justifies hormone testing 
Ask your provider to code appropriately. 
2. Get letters of medical necessity For services insurance won't automatically cover 
(functional medicine, specialized testing, PT), ask your provider to write a letter of 
medical necessity explaining why this is essential for your care. 

===== PDF PAGE 179 =====
161 | P a g e  
 
 
Sometimes insurance will approve with this documentation. 
3. Appeal denials If insurance denies coverage, appeal. First-level appeals are often 
successful if you provide: 
• Letter from provider explaining medical necessity 
• Research supporting the treatment 
• Documentation of failed conventional treatments 
4. Use out-of-network benefits If your plan has out-of-network coverage, you can 
see any provider and submit claims for partial reimbursement. 
Often worth it for specialized care. 
5. Consider cost-sharing programs For people without insurance or with high 
deductibles, health-sharing programs (like Sedera, Medi-Share) can reduce costs for 
major health events. 
6. Check manufacturer discount programs Many medications and supplements 
offer discount programs, coupons, or patient assistance. 
Always ask: "Is there a discount program for this?" 
 
 
The Femtech Revolution (and Its Limitations) 
In the past decade, femtech technology focused on women's health has exploded. 
Apps for period tracking, fertility, menopause symptoms. Wearables that track sleep, 
HRV, temperature. At-home hormone testing. Telemedicine for hormones. 
The promise: democratize access to women's health information and care. 
The reality: mixed results. 
What's working: 

===== PDF PAGE 180 =====
162 | P a g e  
 
 
1. Access Telemedicine has made specialized care available to women in rural areas 
or without local providers. 
2. Data Tracking apps help women identify patterns and advocate for themselves (as 
discussed in Chapter 6). 
3. Community Online platforms connect women with others experiencing similar 
issues, reducing isolation. 
4. Education Apps and platforms provide education women weren't getting from 
doctors. 
 
What's not working: 
1. Data privacy concerns Period tracking apps have been caught selling data. Post-
Roe, this is especially concerning. 
2. Quality varies wildly Some apps/companies are science-based; others peddle 
pseudoscience. 
3. Not accessible to everyone Femtech often requires smartphones, internet access, 
and disposable income. 
4. Doesn't replace systemic change Apps are Band-Aids. We still need medical 
research, insurance coverage, and providers who listen. 
 
Hadiza's recommendation: 
"Use femtech strategically. Track your symptoms. Access telemedicine if you need it. 
But don't let technology replace the fight for systemic change. We need both." 
The Bureau Initiative: The Health Sovereignty Fund Model 
Based on Hadiza's research, the Bureau developed a practical model for women to 
invest in their health strategically. 
The five-year plan: 

===== PDF PAGE 181 =====
163 | P a g e  
 
 
Year 1: Foundation & Assessment 
• Invest in comprehensive testing (hormones, metabolic markers, nutrient 
levels) 
• Find providers who listen 
• Implement Foundation Protocol (Chapter 7) 
• Build emergency fund for health expenses 
Year 2: Optimization 
• Address deficiencies found in Year 1 
• Experiment with protocols (hormonal, metabolic, immune) 
• Continue preventive care 
• Invest in stress management and nervous system work 
Year 3: Maintenance & Prevention 
• Regular testing (2x year) to monitor trends 
• Maintain protocols that worked 
• Focus on sustainability 
• Address any new issues early 
Year 4: Fine-Tuning 
• Optimize based on 3 years of data 
• Prepare for next life phase (perimenopause, menopause, etc.)

• Share knowledge with other women 
Year 5: Long-Term Strategy 
• You now understand your body deeply 
• You have a health team you trust 

===== PDF PAGE 182 =====
164 | P a g e  
 
 
• You've prevented many issues through early intervention 
• You're living the protocol, not fighting your biology 
 
The economic comparison: 
Reactive healthcare (waiting until crisis): 
• Years of suffering 
• Expensive emergency care 
• Long-term medication costs 
• Reduced quality of life and productivity 
• Estimated lifetime cost: $200,000-500,000 
Proactive health sovereignty: 
• Early investment in prevention 
• Reduced need for crisis care

"It's cheaper to invest in your health than to pay for the consequences of neglecting

What You Need to Know 
The healthcare system is economically designed to keep you sick and managing 
symptoms, not to make you healthy. 
This isn't a conspiracy it's how fee-for-service medicine works. Treatment is 
profitable. Prevention is not. 

===== PDF PAGE 183 =====
165 | P a g e  
 
 
You cannot wait for the system to change. It won't change fast enough to help you. 
What you can do: 
• Take control of your health economics 
• Invest strategically in prevention and optimization 
• Learn to navigate insurance and advocate for coverage 
• Use femtech tools wisely 
• Build a health sovereignty fund 
Your health is infrastructure, not luxury. 
Just as you invest in retirement, invest in health. The returns in quality of life, 
productivity, and avoided medical costs are immeasurable. 
And remember Hadiza's lesson: every dollar you spend on prevention saves ten 
dollars on treatment. 
Invest wisely. Invest early. Invest in yourself.

Chapter 14: Building Your Bureau 
Five strangers met in a Facebook group called "PCOS Warriors" in 2023. 
They lived in different cities, different countries even. They'd never met in person. But 
they all shared one thing: they were tired of fighting their hormonal chaos alone. 
"I was in the group for six months before I posted anything," said Maya, 29, from 
Toronto. "I just lurked, reading other women's stories. Then one day I posted about 
my symptoms, and within an hour, four women had responded with their experiences 
and suggestions." 
Those four women became her health cabinet a group that would, over the next two 
years, quite literally save her life. 
"I'd been seeing doctors for PCOS for three years with no improvement," Maya said. 
"But in this group, women shared their test results, their protocols, their practitioners. 
One woman told me about insulin resistance testing that my doctor had never 
mentioned. Another told me about seed cycling. Another connected me with a 
functional medicine doctor in my area." 
Within six months of joining the group and implementing what she learned, Maya's 
cycles regulated. Her acne cleared. Her energy returned. 
"But more than the physical healing," she said, "I stopped feeling alone. I stopped 
thinking I was broken. Because I had four women who'd been where I was and had 
gotten better. They gave me hope when my doctors had given me none." 
This is the power of the Bureau not just the Global Women's Bureau as an institution, 
but the personal bureaus women build for themselves: the community of support, 
knowledge, and collective power. 
Why You Can't Do This Alone (And Shouldn't Have To) 
Healing is a communal act, not an individual one. 
Throughout human history, women healed together. We shared knowledge about 
herbs, about childbirth, about illness. We cared for each other's bodies when the 
medical establishment refused. 

===== PDF PAGE 187 =====
169 | P a g e  
 
 
Then modern medicine professionalized healthcare. Healing became something you 
did alone, in a sterile room, with an expert who had the answers. 
But for women, this model has never worked. 
Because the medical establishment doesn't have answers for most women's health 
issues. Doctors are trained to treat acute disease and manage chronic symptoms. 
They're not trained to optimize health, address root causes, or understand the 
complex interplay of hormones, stress, nutrition, and environment that drives 
women's health. 
So where do we get that knowledge? From each other. 
 
The research supports this: 
Studies show that women with strong social support: 
• Recover faster from illness 
• Have better treatment outcomes 
• Experience less pain 
• Have lower stress hormones 
• Live longer 
Social isolation, conversely, is as harmful to health as smoking 15 cigarettes a day. 
For women specifically, peer support is critical because: 
1. Shared experience validates our reality When doctors dismiss us, other women 
who've been dismissed say: "I believe you. This happened to me too." 
2. Collective knowledge fills gaps in medical care What worked for someone else 
might work for you. We share practitioners, protocols, research. 
3. Accountability and encouragement Healing requires sustained effort. 
Community keeps you going when motivation wanes. 

===== PDF PAGE 188 =====
170 | P a g e  
 
 
4. Emotional processing Health challenges are traumatic. Processing them with 
people who understand is healing in itself. 
5. Political power Alone, we're dismissed. Together, we demand change. 
Building Your Personal Health Team 
Maya's "health cabinet" evolved into a structured support system she now teaches 
other women to build. 
 
The five essential roles: 
1. The Medical Expert This is your functional medicine doctor, integrative MD, or 
naturopath someone with clinical knowledge who orders tests, prescribes when 
needed, but also addresses root causes. 
Where to find: 
• Institute for Functional Medicine provider directory 
• Naturopathic doctor associations 
• Local integrative health centers 
• Referrals from other patients 
What to look for: 
• Listens more than talks 
• Spends adequate time with patients (30+ minutes) 
• Orders comprehensive testing 
• Addresses lifestyle, nutrition, stress 
• Respects your autonomy 
2. The Body Worker This might be a pelvic floor PT, massage therapist, chiropractor, 
or acupuncturist someone who helps you process trauma and tension stored in the 
body. 

===== PDF PAGE 189 =====
171 | P a g e  
 
 
Where to find: 
• Pelvic floor PT: APTA directory 
• Trauma-informed massage: local directories, referrals 
• Acupuncture: NCCAOM directory 
What to look for: 
• Trauma-informed approach 
• Explains what they're doing and why 
• Respects boundaries 
• You feel safe with them 
3. The Mental Health Support Therapist, counselor, or support group facilitator who 
helps you process the emotional aspects of health challenges. 
Where to find: 
• Psychology Today directory (filter for specialties) 
• Trauma therapists (EMDR, somatic experiencing) 
• Support groups (local or online) 
What to look for: 
• Specializes in health-related trauma, medical PTSD, or chronic illness 
• Understands mind-body connection 
• Validates your experience 
4. The Peer Mentor Someone who's been where you are and has made it through 
preferably 1-2 years ahead of you in the journey. 
Where to find: 
• Online support groups for your specific condition 
• Local meetups or health events 

===== PDF PAGE 190 =====
172 | P a g e  
 
 
• Ask in forums: "Anyone willing to mentor?" 
What to look for: 
• Similar health issues or phase of life 
• Has successfully implemented protocols 
• Generous with knowledge 
• Realistic about challenges 
5. The Accountability Partner(s) Friend(s) or fellow travelers who are also working 
on health optimization you check in regularly, share progress and setbacks, keep each 
other going. 
Where to find: 
• Online communities 
• Local women's groups 
• Friends or family who are also health-focused 
What to look for: 
• Committed to their own health journey 
• Non-judgmental 
• Regular check-ins work for both of you 
• Balance of support and accountability 
"You don't need all five immediately," Maya said. "Start with one or two. Build from 
there. It took me a year to assemble my full team." 
Online vs. IRL Support Systems 
Maya's health cabinet was initially entirely online. She'd never met any of the women 
in person. 
"I was worried that made it less 'real,'" she said. "But honestly, the online support was 
more consistent and knowledgeable than any in-person support I'd tried." 

===== PDF PAGE 191 =====
173 | P a g e  
 
 
Benefits of online communities: 
1. Specificity You can find groups for your exact condition, phase of life, or health goal. 
In-person groups are often more general. 
2. Availability 24/7 access. When you're awake at 3 AM with symptoms, someone in 
a different time zone is awake and can respond. 
3. Anonymity Easier to share vulnerable health details with strangers online than 
with people in your daily life. 
4. Diversity Access to experiences and knowledge from around the world, not just 
your local area. 
5. Permanence Written records of conversations, protocols, resources you can refer 
back to. 
 
Limitations of online communities: 
1. Information overload Too many opinions, conflicting advice, can be 
overwhelming. 
2. Quality control Not everyone online has good information. Misinformation 
spreads. 
3. Lack of physical presence No hugs, no eye contact, no energy exchange that 
happens in person. 
4. Echo chambers Can reinforce beliefs (helpful or harmful) without outside 
perspective. 
Benefits of IRL communities: 
1. Physical presence Being seen, held, witnessed in person is powerful. 
2. Accountability Easier to maintain commitments when you'll see people face-to-
face. 

===== PDF PAGE 192 =====
174 | P a g e  
 
 
3. Deeper relationships In-person connection builds intimacy faster for many 
people. 
4. Holistic support Friends can bring you meals, drive you to appointments, sit with 
you in waiting rooms. 
Limitations of IRL communities: 
1. Availability Dependent on geography and schedules. 
2. Less specificity Harder to find people dealing with your exact health issue. 
3. Social dynamics In-person groups can develop cliques, power dynamics that 
online groups avoid. 
Maya's recommendation: Both 
"I have my online PCOS group for specific health knowledge and 24/7 support. And I 
have two local friends I see monthly for in-person connection. Both are essential." 
 
Mentorship Across Generations: What 60-Year-Olds Wish 40-Year-Olds Knew 
One of the most powerful aspects of community is intergenerational knowledge 
transfer. 
The Bureau created a mentorship program pairing women in perimenopause with 
women post-menopause, and women in their 30s with women in their 50s. 
The insights shared were profound. 
What women in their 60s wish they'd known in their 40s: 
"Start strength training NOW. You lose muscle so much faster after menopause. I 
wish I'd built more muscle when it was easier."  — Patricia, 64 
"Hormone therapy isn't dangerous the way we were told. I suffered for 10 years 
unnecessarily because I was scared of HRT. The data is clear now: it's safe for 
most women under 60."  — Susan, 67 

===== PDF PAGE 193 =====
175 | P a g e  
 
 
"Your friendships matter more than your career. I sacrificed relationships for 
work. Now I'm retired, successful, and lonely. Invest in people."  — Diana, 62 
"Track your cycles and symptoms starting in your late 30s. It gives you baseline 
data that's invaluable when perimenopause hits."   
— Margaret, 65 
"Don't wait until you're sick to learn about your body. I knew nothing about 
hormones until I was in crisis. Educate yourself early."   
— Ruth, 61 
"The medical system will not save you. You have to save yourself. Find doctors 
who listen, but don't rely on them to have all the answers."   
— Eleanor, 68 
What women in their 50s wish they'd known in their 30s: 
"Perimenopause starts earlier than you think. My symptoms started at 37. I had 
no idea that was possible."  — Keiko, 52 
"Heal your relationship with your body before menopause. If you hate your body 
at 35, menopause will be brutal."  — Adriana, 54 
"Lift heavy. Build muscle. You'll thank yourself later."  — Simone, 51 
"Find your people. Community is medicine. I isolated myself and it made 
everything worse."  — Fatima, 53 
"Your metabolism doesn't just slow down on its own. It slows because you lose 
muscle and hormones. Both are addressable."  — Linda, 56 
What women in their 40s wish they'd known in their 20s: 
"Your 20s are when you set the foundation. What you do then determines your 
health in your 40s, 50s, and beyond."  — Priya, 42 
"Birth control masks problems. When I went off at 35 to try to conceive, I 
discovered I had PCOS. I'd had it for years but BC hid it."  — Jessica, 44 

===== PDF PAGE 194 =====
176 | P a g e  
 
 
"You don't bounce back forever. I could get away with terrible sleep and eating 
in my 20s. Not anymore. Start good habits young."  — Zara, 41 
"Stress compounds. Every stressful period I didn't recover from properly 
contributed to my burnout at 38. Recover fully before taking on more."  — Lin, 43 
This wisdom earned through years of lived experience is what community provides. 
You don't have to learn everything the hard way if women ahead of you share what 
they know. 
 
The Bureau Model: The Peer Health Council Structure 
Based on Maya's experience and the Bureau's research on effective health 
communities, they developed the Peer Health Council model. 
The structure: 
Small groups of 4-6 women: 
• Meet weekly or bi-weekly (virtual or in-person) 
• Similar health goals or challenges 
• Rotating facilitator (everyone leads sometimes) 
Each meeting has structure: 
1. Check-in (15 min) 
• How's everyone feeling? 
• What's working, what's challenging? 
• Any urgent needs or wins to celebrate? 
2. Education (20 min) 
• One person presents something they learned (research, protocol, practitioner 
recommendation) 
• Group discusses and asks questions 

===== PDF PAGE 195 =====
177 | P a g e  
 
 
3. Troubleshooting (20 min) 
• One person presents a challenge they're facing 
• Group brainstorms solutions, shares experiences 
• No advice-giving, just sharing: "Here's what worked for me" 
4. Accountability (10 min) 
• Each person shares one commitment for the next week/two weeks 
• Group witnesses and supports 
5. Closing (5 min) 
• Gratitude, affirmations, mutual support 
Ground rules: 
• Confidentiality (what's shared stays in the group) 
• No medical advice (share experiences, not prescriptions) 
• Respect differences (what works for one may not work for another) 
• No judgment (everyone's journey is valid) 
• Hold space (listen more than fix) 
 
Maya's council: 
She started her Peer Health Council with four women from the PCOS Facebook group. 
They've been meeting for two years. 
"We've seen each other through so much," Maya said. "Diagnosis, treatment, setbacks, 
victories. We know each other's lab values, symptoms, cycles. We've built collective 
knowledge that no individual doctor has." 
One woman in her group reversed her PCOS. Another got pregnant after three years 
of trying. Another discovered she had celiac disease that had been missed for a 
decade. 

===== PDF PAGE 196 =====
178 | P a g e  
 
 
"We saved each other," Maya said simply. "Not doctors. Each other." 
 
What You Need to Know 
You cannot heal alone. Humans are not built for isolation, especially not in crisis. 
Build your Bureau: 
1. Find your people 
• Online communities for your specific condition 
• Local support groups or meetup groups 
• Friends or family who are health-focused 
• Professionals who treat you as a partner 
2. Structure your support 
• Medical expert who listens 
• Body worker for somatic healing 
• Mental health support for emotional processing 
• Peer mentor who's been where you are 
• Accountability partners on the journey with you 
3. Give as much as you receive Community isn't one-directional. Share what you 
learn. Support others. Mentorship flows both ways. 
4. Set boundaries Not every community is healthy. Leave groups that make you feel 
worse, drain you, or spread misinformation. 
5. Show up consistently Community requires investment. You get out what you put 
in. 
And remember Maya's lesson: healing is communal. 
The medical system isolated us, convinced us our health is between us and our doctor. 

===== PDF PAGE 197 =====
179 | P a g e  
 
 
But women have always healed together. We've shared knowledge, held each other, 
witnessed each other's pain and victories. 
That's not alternative medicine. That's how healing has worked for millennia. 
Build your Bureau. Find your people. Share your story. 
You are not alone. You never were.

Chapter 15: The Next Generation Protocol 
Amara Chen was 12 years old when she got her first period. 
She was at school. She bled through her jeans. A boy pointed and laughed. She spent 
the rest of the day in the nurse's office, humiliated and confused, wearing borrowed 
sweatpants. 
When she got home, her mother Dr. Zara Chen, the same researcher who founded the 
Bureau's Bio-Harmonization protocols sat her down. 
"I'm sorry you had that experience," Zara said. "And I'm sorry I didn't prepare you 
better. But we're going to make sure you have the education I never got. You're going 
to understand your body in a way most women don't learn until they're 40." 
That conversation began what Zara now calls "The Next Generation Protocol" a 
comprehensive education program designed to give young women the knowledge 
that could prevent decades of suffering. 
"I didn't want my daughter to spend years being dismissed by doctors like I was," Zara 
told me. "I didn't want her to develop PCOS or thyroid disease or burnout and only 
discover it when it became a crisis. I wanted her to have the tools to understand and 
advocate for her body from the beginning." 
Five years later, Amara is 17. She tracks her cycle, understands her hormonal phases, 
knows how to read her own lab results, and has already identified that she has insulin 
resistance early enough to prevent PCOS from fully developing. 
"My friends think I'm weird," Amara said, laughing. "They're like, 'Why do you know 
your fasting insulin level?' But I've seen what happens when women don't know this 
stuff. I've heard my mom's patients' stories. I don't want to be them." 
What Girls Need to Know at 12, 16, 22 (That We Didn't) 
Zara developed the education protocol in collaboration with educators, adolescent 
health specialists, and most importantly, young women themselves. 
The framework is age-appropriate, science-based, and empowering not fear-
based. 

===== PDF PAGE 201 =====
183 | P a g e  
 
 
 
Age 12-14: Menarche and Body Literacy 
What they need to know: 
1. Periods are normal, but severe pain is not 
"The biggest lie we tell girls," Zara said, "is that periods are supposed to hurt. Mild 
cramping is normal. Debilitating pain, vomiting, missing school that's not normal. 
That's often endometriosis or another condition." 
What to teach: 
• Mild cramping, bloating, and mood changes are normal 
• Pain that interferes with daily life (missing school, needing to lie down, 
vomiting) is NOT normal and should be evaluated 
• Track your periods: when they start, how long they last, how heavy the flow is, 
any symptoms 
Why this matters: Early recognition of abnormal symptoms can lead to diagnosis of 
endometriosis, PCOS, or other conditions years earlier preventing complications and 
preserving fertility. 
 
 
2. Your cycle is a vital sign 
"I teach girls that their cycle tells them about their overall health," Zara said. "Just like 
we monitor heart rate and blood pressure, we should monitor cycles." 
What to teach: 
• A healthy cycle is 25-35 days 
• Bleeding should last 3-7 days 
• You should need to change a pad or tampon every 3-4 hours (not every hour, 
not once a day) 

===== PDF PAGE 202 =====
184 | P a g e  
 
 
• Severe PMS is not normal it's a sign of hormonal imbalance 
• Missing periods (when not pregnant) is a sign something needs attention 
Why this matters: Many conditions (PCOS, thyroid disease, eating disorders, stress-
related amenorrhea) first show up as cycle irregularities. Catching them early 
prevents long-term consequences. 
3. Bodies change at different rates comparison is useless 
What to teach: 
• Puberty happens on a spectrum (ages 8-16) 
• Breast development, body shape, height, period timing all vary widely 
• Social media shows heavily filtered, photoshopped, often adult bodies not 
realistic for teens 
• Your body is changing to support your health, not to look a certain way 
Why this matters: Preventing body image issues and disordered eating before they 
start is far easier than treating them later. 
4. Periods don't need to be hidden or shameful 
What to teach: 
• Menstruation is a normal biological process 
• It's okay to say "I have my period" instead of using euphemisms 
• It's okay to ask for what you need (pain relief, rest, understanding from 
teachers) 
Why this matters: Period shame keeps girls from seeking help when something is 
wrong. Normalizing periods enables early intervention. 
 
Amara's experience at 12: 

===== PDF PAGE 203 =====
185 | P a g e  
 
 
"When I got my period, my mom taught me how to track it in an app. She showed me 
what to look for cycle length, flow, symptoms. She told me what was normal and what 
wasn't. She made it feel like information, not something embarrassing." 
By 13, Amara noticed her cycles were irregular sometimes 25 days, sometimes 45 
days. 
"My mom said that's common in the first year, but we should keep tracking. By 14, 
they still weren't regular, so we did blood work. Turns out my fasting insulin was 
elevated. Early sign of insulin resistance." 
They caught it early enough to intervene with diet and lifestyle changes before PCOS 
fully developed. 
"If I hadn't been tracking, if I'd just been told 'irregular periods are normal for teens,' 
I might not have known until I was trying to get pregnant in my 20s or 30s." 
 
Age 16-18: Understanding Your Hormones and Making Informed Decisions 
What they need to know: 
1. Birth control is a tool, not a cure 
"So many girls are put on birth control for acne, irregular periods, or painful periods," 
Zara said. "And birth control can be helpful. But it masks underlying problems it 
doesn't fix them." 
What to teach: 
• Birth control regulates cycles by overriding your natural hormones 
• It can be helpful for preventing pregnancy and managing symptoms 
• But it doesn't cure PCOS, endometriosis, or hormonal imbalances it covers 
them up 
• If you go on BC, know why you're on it, and have a plan for eventually 
addressing root causes 

===== PDF PAGE 204 =====
186 | P a g e  
 
 
• BC has side effects (mood changes, nutrient depletion, blood clot risk) 
understand them before starting 
Why this matters: Many women spend 10-15 years on birth control without knowing 
they have underlying conditions. When they go off to try to conceive, they discover 
PCOS, endometriosis, or other issues that have worsened untreated. 
2. Your body is not the same every day of the month 
What to teach: 
• You have four hormonal phases in your cycle (follicular, ovulatory, luteal, 
menstrual) 
• Energy, mood, appetite, sleep needs, and even cognitive function change across 
these phases 
• Working WITH your cycle instead of against it makes life easier 
• Schedule important events (exams, presentations, competitions) during your 
follicular or ovulatory phase when energy is highest 
• Be gentler with yourself during late luteal and menstrual phases 
Why this matters: Girls who understand their cycles can optimize performance and 
reduce the frustration of feeling "off" at certain times of the month. 
3. Mental health and hormones are connected 
What to teach: 
• Hormones affect mood, anxiety, and cognitive function 
• If you notice your mental health symptoms are cyclical (worse at certain times 
of your cycle), that's hormonal 
• PMDD (premenstrual dysphoric disorder) is real and treatable 
• Not all depression/anxiety is purely psychological sometimes it's hormonal 
• Both therapy AND hormone support might be needed 

===== PDF PAGE 205 =====
187 | P a g e  
 
 
Why this matters: Many teenage girls are put on antidepressants or anxiety 
medication when the root cause is hormonal. Addressing the hormones can eliminate 
the need for psychiatric medication in some cases. 
4. Disordered eating and your cycle 
What to teach: 
• Restrictive dieting can shut down your period (hypothalamic amenorrhea) 
• Missing periods is not "convenient" it's a sign your body is stressed 
• You need adequate calories, protein, and fat for healthy hormone production 
• "Clean eating" and orthorexia can be just as harmful as traditional eating 
disorders 
Why this matters: Eating disorders and disordered eating are epidemic in teenage 
girls. Understanding the hormonal consequences (loss of period, bone density loss, 
future fertility issues) can motivate recovery. 
 
Amara's experience at 16: 
"My mom had 'the birth control talk' with me when I was 16 not about whether I 
should take it, but about understanding what it does. She explained that if I ever 
decided to go on it, I should know it's covering symptoms, not fixing problems. And 
that I should address root causes too." 
Amara also learned to track her energy and mood across her cycle. 
"I noticed I felt more social and outgoing during the first half of my cycle, and more 
introverted during the second half. Once I knew that was normal, I stopped thinking 
something was wrong with me. I just planned around it scheduled social stuff for 
when I naturally wanted to be social." 
 
Age 20-25: Prevention and Body Literacy 

===== PDF PAGE 206 =====
188 | P a g e  
 
 
What they need to know: 
1. Your 20s are when you set the foundation 
What to teach: 
• What you do in your 20s determines your health in your 40s, 50s, 60s 
• Build muscle now (it's easier in your 20s and protects against metabolic 
disease later) 
• Establish healthy sleep patterns (chronic sleep deprivation accumulates) 
• Learn stress management skills before burnout happens 
• Understand your baseline health (get comprehensive labs in your early 20s to 
know what's normal for you) 
Why this matters: So many women only start paying attention to health when 
something goes wrong. By then, damage has accumulated. Prevention is exponentially 
easier than treatment. 
2. Not all healthcare providers are equal 
What to teach: 
• You're allowed to fire doctors who don't listen 
• Bring data to appointments (symptom tracking, lab results, research) 
• Ask questions and expect answers 
• Get second opinions 
• You are the CEO of your health 
Why this matters: Women who learn to advocate for themselves early avoid years of 
medical dismissal. 
3. Stress, sleep, and nutrition are not optional 
What to teach: 
• You can't "hustle" your way through hormonal dysfunction 

===== PDF PAGE 207 =====
189 | P a g e  
 
 
• All-nighters, chronic stress, and surviving on coffee will catch up with you 
• Your body keeps score 
• Recovery is not weakness it's essential 
Why this matters: The "girl boss" culture glorifies burnout. Teaching young women 
that their bodies have limits prevents the crashes that derail careers and health. 
4. Fertility awareness is empowering 
What to teach: 
• Understanding your fertile window and cycle gives you agency 
• Fertility awareness methods (when learned properly) can prevent or achieve 
pregnancy 
• Tracking your cycle teaches you about your body 
• You don't have to be trying to conceive to care about ovulation it's a sign of 
metabolic health 
Why this matters: Many women know nothing about their fertility until they're 
trying to conceive and struggling. Understanding it early allows for proactive 
intervention if needed. 
5. Baseline lab work in your 20s 
What to teach: Get comprehensive labs in your early 20s to establish your baseline: 
• Complete thyroid panel (TSH, Free T3, Free T4, antibodies) 
• Fasting glucose and insulin 
• Vitamin D, B12, ferritin 
• Sex hormones (estrogen, progesterone, testosterone on appropriate cycle 
days) 
• Inflammatory markers (hsCRP) 

===== PDF PAGE 208 =====
190 | P a g e  
 
 
Why this matters: Knowing what's normal for you makes it easier to identify when 
something's changing. Many women don't get labs until they're sick by then, there's 
no baseline for comparison. 
 
Raising Boys Who Don't Gaslight Women's Pain 
"This isn't just about educating girls," Zara emphasized. "We have to educate boys too. 
Because boys become men, and men become doctors, partners, fathers. If we don't 
teach them to take women's pain seriously, nothing changes." 
 
 
 
What boys need to learn: 
1. Women's pain is real 
What to teach: 
• Periods can be painful (don't mock or minimize) 
• Women aren't being dramatic when they report symptoms 
• Believe women when they say something hurts 
• Women's medical experiences are often dismissed don't add to that 
2. Basic biology 
What to teach: 
• How periods work 
• Hormonal cycles affect mood and energy (it's not "being crazy") 
• Pregnancy and childbirth are physically demanding 
• Menopause is a real, significant hormonal transition 

===== PDF PAGE 209 =====
191 | P a g e  
 
 
Why this matters: Men who understand basic female biology are better partners, 
better fathers, better healthcare providers. 
3. Emotional labor is labor 
What to teach: 
• Managing a household (even if you're not doing the physical tasks) is work 
• Remembering appointments, birthdays, schedules is work 
• Emotional support and relationship maintenance is work 
• Women often carry this invisible burden recognize and share it 
4. Advocate for the women in your life 
What to teach: 
• If a woman is dismissed by a doctor, speak up: "I'm concerned her symptoms 
aren't being taken seriously" 
• Support women taking time for health appointments, rest, recovery 
• Don't pressure women to "tough it out" or minimize their needs 
 
Zara's approach with her son (Amara's younger brother): 
"When he was 13, I sat him down and explained periods. He was embarrassed, but I 
told him: 'Half the population deals with this monthly. You need to understand it.' I 
explained cramping, hormonal changes, why his sister might need a heating pad or 
want to skip the gym some days." 
She also talks to him about how women are treated in healthcare. 
"I've told him stories sanitized for his age of patients I've had who were dismissed by 
male doctors. I want him to grow up knowing that women's pain is systematically 
minimized, so he doesn't contribute to that." 
 

===== PDF PAGE 210 =====
192 | P a g e  
 
 
The Education Revolution: Integrating Female Physiology into Schools 
Currently, sex education in most schools consists of: 
• Basic anatomy 
• How pregnancy happens 
• STI prevention 
• Maybe how to use a condom or discussion of consent 
What's missing: 
• How hormonal cycles work 
• What's normal vs. what's a red flag 
• How to track your cycle 
• How stress, sleep, and nutrition affect hormones 
• What conditions like PCOS, endometriosis, and thyroid disease look like 
"Imagine if we taught this in 8th grade health class," Zara said. "Imagine if every girl 
learned to track her cycle, recognize red flags, advocate for herself. How many years 
of suffering could we prevent?" 
 
The Bureau's Vision: The 2035 Curriculum Framework 
The Bureau is working with educators to develop a comprehensive health curriculum 
that includes women's hormonal health. 
Proposed curriculum: 
8th Grade (Age 13-14): 
• Menstrual cycle basics 
• What's normal vs. red flags 
• How to track your cycle 

===== PDF PAGE 211 =====
193 | P a g e  
 
 
• Body literacy and self-advocacy 
10th Grade (Age 15-16): 
• Hormonal phases and cycle syncing 
• Birth control options and considerations 
• Mental health and hormones 
• Disordered eating and hormonal consequences 
12th Grade (Age 17-18): 
• Comprehensive health: stress, sleep, nutrition, movement 
• How to advocate in healthcare settings 
• Understanding lab results 
• Fertility awareness 
• Setting health foundation for adulthood 
Why this matters: 
"Right now, we're sending girls into the world with zero understanding of their 
bodies," Zara said. "Then we're surprised when they spend their 20s and 30s 
struggling with preventable conditions." 
Education is prevention. And prevention is far cheaper emotionally, physically, 
financially than treatment. 
 
What Amara Wants Other Girls to Know 
I asked Amara what she'd tell her peers girls who haven't had the education she 
received. 
"I'd tell them: your body isn't a mystery. It's not supposed to be this confusing. You're 
not supposed to just suffer through periods or feel terrible and not know why." 

===== PDF PAGE 212 =====

She paused. "I'd tell them to start tracking their cycles now. Not because they're trying 
to get pregnant, but because it teaches you about your body. You start to see patterns. 
You understand yourself better." 
"And I'd tell them: you're allowed to ask questions. You're allowed to push back when 
a doctor dismisses you. You're allowed to take your health seriously even if you're 
young." 
"Your body is yours. Learn how it works. Take care of it. Don't wait until something's 
wrong." 
 
What You Need to Know 
The next generation doesn't have to suffer the way we did. 
If you have daughters, nieces, students, or young women in your life: 
• Teach them body literacy 
• Normalize periods and hormones 
• Show them how to track their cycles 
• Educate them about red flags 
• Model self-advocacy 
• Give them the knowledge we didn't have 
If you have sons: 
• Teach them about female biology 
• Show them how to support the women in their lives 
• Model taking women's pain seriously 
• Raise them to be advocates, not dismissers 
The cycle of medical dismissal, delayed diagnosis, and preventable suffering 
ends when we educate the next generation. 

===== PDF PAGE 213 =====
195 | P a g e  
 
 
Not in medical schools (though that needs to change too). In homes. In conversations 
with young people. 
One girl at a time. One boy at a time. 
Until an entire generation grows up knowing what we had to learn the hard way.
