Chapter 16: The Global Women's Health Mandate 
In 2024, the Global Women's Bureau convened the first-ever International Summit on 
Women's Health Equity in Geneva, Switzerland. 
Healthcare ministers, researchers, advocates, and patients from 87 countries 
gathered for three days to answer one question: 
 
Why, in 2024, do women still receive inadequate, dismissive, and often harmful 
healthcare and what are we going to do about it? 
The summit resulted in the Global Women's Health Mandate: a 10-point declaration 
of what every woman on Earth is entitled to in healthcare. 
It wasn't binding. No country was required to adopt it. 
But something remarkable happened. Within 18 months, 12 countries had 
implemented at least portions of the Mandate into national health policy. 
And in those countries, women's health outcomes improved dramatically. 
 
What's Working: Models from Around the World 
Before presenting their vision for the future, the Bureau's research team studied 
countries that were doing women's health well. 
 
What they found: 
Rwanda's Maternal Health Model 
The innovation: Community health workers (mostly women) in every village, trained 
to: 
• Monitor pregnant women throughout pregnancy 
• Identify complications early 

===== PDF PAGE 217 =====
199 | P a g e  
 
 
• Refer to clinics when needed 
• Provide postpartum support 
The results: 
• Maternal mortality dropped 77% in 10 years 
• Nearly 100% of births now attended by skilled providers 
• Postpartum depression identified and treated early 
Why it works: 
• Health support is local, accessible, and culturally appropriate 
• Women supporting women 
• Prevention and early intervention, not crisis management 
What other countries can learn: Community-based care, training non-physicians to 
provide frontline health support, and investing in prevention 
 
Iceland's Menopause Leave 
The innovation: Women experiencing severe menopause symptoms can take 
medical leave (up to 3 months), paid at 80% of salary. 
The rationale: Menopause affects 100% of women. For approximately 25% of 
women, symptoms are severe enough to impact work capacity. Instead of forcing 
women to choose between suffering at work or losing income, support them during 
this transition. 
 
The results: 
• Women stay in workforce instead of quitting 
• Productivity improves (women return from leave more functional than they 
were while suffering) 

===== PDF PAGE 218 =====
200 | P a g e  
 
 
• Reduced long-term healthcare costs (symptoms addressed early rather than 
worsening) 
• Economic analysis shows ROI of 3:1 (every dollar spent on leave saves $3 in 
lost productivity and healthcare costs) 
Why it works: Recognizes menopause as a significant health transition requiring 
support, not something women should "tough out" 
What other countries can learn: Supporting women through natural life transitions 
keeps them healthy and economically productive 
 
South Korea's Period-Tracking Health Integration 
The innovation: National health system provides free period-tracking app integrated 
with medical records. Women can: 
• Track cycles, symptoms, and triggers 
• Share data with healthcare providers with one click 
• Receive alerts if patterns suggest conditions (PCOS, endometriosis, thyroid 
issues) 
• Get automatic referrals to specialists based on patterns 
 
 
The results: 
• Average time to diagnosis for PCOS dropped from 2.3 years to 8 months 
• Endometriosis diagnosis time dropped from 7.5 years to 2.1 years 
• 34% reduction in emergency room visits for menstrual-related issues 
• Women report feeling more empowered and informed about their health 
Why it works: 

===== PDF PAGE 219 =====
201 | P a g e  
 
 
• Leverages technology women are already using (smartphones) 
• Turns data into actionable health insights 
• Connects women with appropriate care faster 
What other countries can learn: Digital health tools can democratize access to 
knowledge and accelerate diagnosis 
 
Portugal's Endometriosis Centers of Excellence 
The innovation: Designated endometriosis centers in every region staffed by 
multidisciplinary teams: 
• Gynaecologists specializing in endo 
• Pain management specialists 
• Pelvic floor physical therapists 
• Mental health support 
• Nutritionists 
All care is covered by national health system. 
The results: 
• Average time to diagnosis dropped from 7 years to 1.5 years 
• Surgical outcomes improved (specialized surgeons have better results) 
• Quality of life scores for endo patients increased by 61% 
• 43% reduction in need for repeat surgeries 
Why it works: 
• Specialization improves outcomes 
• Multidisciplinary care addresses all aspects of complex conditions 
• Patients don't have to cobble together their own care teams 

===== PDF PAGE 220 =====
202 | P a g e  
 
 
What other countries can learn: Investing in centers of excellence for complex 
women's health conditions improves outcomes and reduces long-term costs 
 
The Netherlands' Comprehensive Perimenopause Care 
The innovation: All women over 40 receive: 
• Annual perimenopause screening (symptom questionnaire, hormone testing if 
indicated) 
• Access to menopause specialists (covered by insurance) 
• Education program on what to expect and how to optimize health through 
transition 
• Hormone therapy covered if appropriate 
The results: 
• 87% of women report feeling prepared for perimenopause (vs. 23% globally) 
• Quality of life during perimenopause significantly higher than in countries 
without programs 
• Fewer women leaving workforce due to symptoms 
• Better long-term health outcomes (cardiovascular, bone density, cognitive) 
Why it works: 
• Proactive, not reactive 
• Education and support before crisis 
• Destigmatizes hormone therapy 
What other countries can learn: Perimenopause is a predictable transition affecting 
all women universal screening and support should be standard 
 
What's Not Working: Lessons from Failures 

===== PDF PAGE 221 =====
203 | P a g e  
 
 
The Bureau also studied failed initiatives to understand what doesn't work. 
Pink-washing without substance: 
Many companies and governments have launched "women's health initiatives" that 
are more marketing than medicine. 
Example: A major health system advertised "women-centered care" but didn't: 
• Train providers in women's health 
• Increase appointment times 
• Address dismissive provider behavior 
• Cover comprehensive testing 
Result: No improvement in outcomes. Women felt more betrayed than before 
(because promises were made and not kept). 
Lesson: Words without action are harmful. Don't claim to prioritize women's health 
unless you're willing to invest in systemic change. 
 
Technology without support: 
Several countries rolled out digital health apps for women without: 
• Ensuring data privacy 
• Integrating with actual healthcare 
• Training providers to use the data 
• Making apps accessible to all women (language, literacy, internet access) 
Result: Low adoption, no improvement in outcomes, data breaches that harmed 
women. 
Lesson: Technology is a tool, not a solution. It only works if integrated into 
comprehensive care and accessible to all. 
Hormones as the only answer: 

===== PDF PAGE 222 =====
204 | P a g e  
 
 
Some systems swung too far toward hormone replacement as a cure-all, without: 
• Addressing root causes (stress, nutrition, lifestyle) 
• Screening for contraindications 
• Providing non-hormonal options 
• Supporting women who can't or won't take hormones 
Result: Some women benefited, but many felt pressured or had adverse effects. Didn't 
address broader systemic issues. 
Lesson: Hormones are one tool, not the only tool. Comprehensive care requires 
multiple approaches. 
 
The Role of AI and Personalized Medicine (With Ethical Guardrails) 
The Bureau's vision for the future includes leveraging AI and precision medicine but 
with strong ethical frameworks. 
Where AI can help: 
1. Pattern recognition in symptoms 
AI can analyze thousands of symptom patterns and identify conditions faster than 
individual doctors. 
Example: AI analyzing period-tracking data can flag potential endometriosis, PCOS, or 
thyroid issues based on patterns prompting earlier testing and diagnosis. 
2. Personalized treatment recommendations 
Based on genetic data, microbiome analysis, and biomarkers, AI can suggest 
personalized protocols instead of one-size-fits-all approaches. 
Example: Determining which women will benefit from specific types of hormone 
therapy based on their genetic variants. 
3. Reducing diagnostic bias 

===== PDF PAGE 223 =====
205 | P a g e  
 
 
AI doesn't have implicit biases (gender, race, age). When trained on diverse datasets, 
it can provide more equitable diagnostic suggestions. 
Where AI needs guardrails: 
1. Data privacy 
Women's health data is deeply personal. Without strong privacy protections, it can be 
used to: 
• Discriminate (insurance, employment) 
• Criminalize (pregnancy tracking post-Roe) 
• Exploit (selling data to third parties) 
Guardrail: Strict data ownership laws. Women own their data. Consent required for 
any use. Severe penalties for breaches. 
2. Algorithmic bias 
If AI is trained primarily on white, Western women's data, it will perform poorly for 
women of color, women in other countries, women with atypical presentations. 
Guardrail: Require diverse, representative training datasets. Regular auditing for 
bias. Transparency in how algorithms work. 
3. Replacement of human care 
AI should augment, not replace, human providers. Women need to be seen, heard, 
witnessed not just diagnosed by an algorithm. 
Guardrail: AI as a tool for providers, not a replacement. Always human oversight. 
Patients have right to human consultation. 
4. Corporate capture 
If AI health tools are owned by for-profit companies, profit motives will override 
health outcomes. 
Guardrail: Non-profit or public ownership of health AI. Open-source algorithms. 
Community oversight. 

===== PDF PAGE 224 =====
206 | P a g e  
 
 
 
The 10-Point Global Women's Health Mandate 
Based on what's working, what's not, and the potential of emerging technologies, the 
Bureau issued this declaration: 
1. UNIVERSAL EDUCATION 
Every girl receives comprehensive education on: 
• Menstrual cycle and hormonal health 
• Body literacy and self-advocacy 
• Nutrition, stress, sleep, and their impact on health 
• Red flags and when to seek care 
Timeframe: Implementation in schools by 2030 
 
2. DIAGNOSTIC EQUITY 
Maximum acceptable time to diagnosis: 
• Endometriosis: 1 year from onset of symptoms 
• PCOS: 6 months from symptom onset 
• Autoimmune disease: 1 year from symptom onset 
• Perimenopause: Immediate recognition and support 
Providers receive training to recognize symptoms and refer appropriately. 
Timeframe: Establish diagnostic benchmarks by 2027, achieve targets by 2032 
 
3. COMPREHENSIVE CARE 
Women receive: 
• Annual comprehensive hormonal health screening starting at age 30 

===== PDF PAGE 225 =====
207 | P a g e  
 
 
• Access to multidisciplinary care for complex conditions 
• Mental health support as part of standard care 
• Time-adequate appointments (minimum 30 minutes for complex issues) 
Timeframe: Universal coverage by 2035 
 
4. PAIN EQUITY 
Women's pain is: 
• Believed and taken seriously 
• Treated with same urgency and medication as men's pain 
• Investigated thoroughly before being attributed to psychological causes 
Providers receive mandatory training on pain bias and women's health. 
Timeframe: Training programs implemented by 2028 
 
5. MENOPAUSE SUPPORT 
All women over 40 receive: 
• Education on what to expect 
• Symptom screening 
• Access to hormone therapy if appropriate 
• Workplace accommodations if needed (flexible schedule, medical leave) 
Timeframe: National programs in place by 2030 
 
6. RESEARCH EQUITY 
Women's health research receives: 

===== PDF PAGE 226 =====
208 | P a g e  
 
 
• At least 10% of total health research funding (up from current <1%) 
• Requirement for clinical trials to include adequate numbers of women 
• Mandate to analyze results by sex and gender 
• Focus on conditions that primarily affect women (not just reproductive 
cancers) 
Timeframe: Funding targets met by 2030 
 
7. DATA SOVEREIGNTY 
Women own their health data: 
• Explicit consent required for any use 
• Right to access, download, and delete their data 
• Severe penalties for unauthorized use or sale 
• Data cannot be used to discriminate (insurance, employment, legal) 
Timeframe: Data protection laws enacted by 2027 
 
8. PROVIDER ACCOUNTABILITY 
Healthcare providers are held accountable for: 
• Dismissive or discriminatory behavior 
• Failure to investigate symptoms thoroughly 
• Delayed diagnosis when reasonable standards of care were not met 
Patients have clear pathways for complaints and recourse. 
Timeframe: Accountability mechanisms by 2028 
 
9. ECONOMIC SUPPORT 

===== PDF PAGE 227 =====
209 | P a g e  
 
 
Women are supported financially during health transitions: 
• Paid medical leave for menopause, pregnancy complications, chronic illness 
• Insurance coverage for comprehensive testing, functional medicine, 
multidisciplinary care 
• No out-of-pocket costs for preventive care 
Timeframe: Insurance mandates by 2030, paid leave by 2032 
 
 
10. COMMUNITY INTEGRATION 
Health systems recognize that healing is communal: 
• Support for peer health groups 
• Community health workers (especially in underserved areas) 
• Integration of traditional healing practices (when safe and evidence-based) 
• Family and community involvement in care when appropriate 
Timeframe: Community health programs expanded by 2030 
 
The Countries Leading the Way 
As of 2026, 12 countries have begun implementing portions of the Mandate: 
Comprehensive implementation (5+ points): 
• Iceland 
• Rwanda 
• Portugal 
• The Netherlands 
• New Zealand 

===== PDF PAGE 228 =====
210 | P a g e  
 
 
Partial implementation (2-4 points): 
• South Korea 
• Canada 
• Scotland 
• Australia 
• Uruguay 
• Costa Rica 
• Finland 
The early results are promising: 
In countries implementing 5+ points of the Mandate: 
• Time to diagnosis for common conditions decreased 52% 
• Women's satisfaction with healthcare increased 67% 
• Maternal mortality decreased 34% 
• Women's workforce participation increased 8% 
• Long-term healthcare costs decreased 23% (due to prevention) 
"This isn't theoretical," Hadiza said. "We have real data from real countries showing 
that when we invest in women's health, everyone benefits. Women live healthier lives. 
Economies grow. Healthcare costs decrease." 
 
The Resistance: Why Change Is Slow 
Despite the evidence, most countries have been slow to adopt the Mandate. 
The barriers: 
1. Economic "We can't afford it" (despite evidence it saves money long-term) 
2. Political Women's health isn't a priority for male-dominated governments 

===== PDF PAGE 229 =====
211 | P a g e  
 
 
3. Cultural Deep-rooted beliefs that women's suffering is normal or exaggerated 
4. Medical establishment resistance Requires admitting systemic failures and 
changing how medicine is practiced 
5. Pharmaceutical industry Chronic management is more profitable than 
prevention 
"The resistance isn't logical," Hadiza said. "It's ideological. It's the same resistance 
women have always faced when we demand to be treated as fully human." 
But she's optimistic. 
"Look at the 12 countries that have started. Ten years ago, none of this existed. Change 
is happening. It's slow, but it's real." 
 
What You Can Do 
You don't need to wait for national governments to implement the Mandate. 
Individual action: 
1. Vote with your voice 
• Contact your representatives 
• Demand women's health policy 
• Support candidates who prioritize it 
2. Vote with your wallet 
• Support companies that offer menopause leave, comprehensive health 
coverage 
• Boycott companies that exploit women's health data 
 
3. Share your story 
• Medical trauma, diagnostic delays, dismissal these stories matter 

===== PDF PAGE 230 =====
212 | P a g e  
 
 
• When enough women speak up, silence becomes impossible 
4. Build your Bureau Create the support systems the healthcare system won't 
provide 
5. Educate the next generation Give young people the knowledge we didn't have

"Imagine," Zara said at the closing of the summit, "a world where: 
• Girls learn about their bodies in school, not from Instagram

• Endometriosis is diagnosed in months, not years 
• Perimenopause is met with support, not dismissal

That world is possible. We have the knowledge. We have the technology. We have the 
economic evidence. 
What we need is the political will. 
And that comes from us. From women refusing to accept inadequate care. From 
women educating our daughters and sons. From women building the systems we need 
when institutions fail us. 
The Global Women's Health Mandate is not a document from an institution. It's a 
declaration from us to the world: 

===== PDF PAGE 231 =====

We will no longer accept being dismissed. We will no longer accept delayed diagnosis. 
We will no longer accept suffering as normal. 
We demand equity. We demand dignity. We demand health. 
And we will build it ourselves if we have to."

EPILOGUE: Your Body, Your Bureau 
Three years after I started the Foundation Protocol, I ran a half-marathon. 
At 41 years old. In perimenopause. After being told by multiple doctors that my 
"hormones wouldn't allow" the kind of training required. 
My finish time wasn't impressive 2 hours and 18 minutes. But I wasn't running for 
time. I was running because three years earlier, I could barely walk up stairs without 
getting winded. 
I was running because my body the body I'd spent years fighting, resenting, and trying 
to force into submission had become my partner instead of my enemy. 
I crossed the finish line crying. 
Not from pain or exhaustion, but from gratitude. Gratitude for the women who came 
before me and documented what worked. Gratitude for the researchers who refused 

===== PDF PAGE 233 =====
215 | P a g e  
 
 
to accept "it's all in your head" as an answer. Gratitude for my own body, which never 
gave up on me even when I gave up on it. 
This book is my way of paying that forward. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The Final Lesson: This Is Not About Perfection 
In the three years since I started this journey, I've had setbacks. 
There was the month I got COVID and my carefully built routines fell apart. The time 
I went through a devastating breakup and stress sent my cortisol through the roof. 
The period where work demands meant I slept 5 hours a night for two weeks and my 
cycle disappeared. 
I'm not perfect. My protocol isn't perfect. My body isn't perfect. 
But here's what I've learned: perfection isn't the goal. Partnership is. 
Partnership means: 
• Listening when your body says it needs rest 
• Adjusting when something isn't working 
• Forgiving yourself when you fall off track 
• Getting back on without shame or judgment 
It means understanding that your body is doing its best with the resources you give 
it. 

===== PDF PAGE 234 =====
216 | P a g e  
 
 
Feed it well, and it thrives. Starve it, stress it, ignore it, and it struggles. 
That's not failure. That's biology. 
 
Your n=1 Experiment Matters 
Scientists talk about "n=1" experiments studies with a single subject. They're 
considered less valuable than large-scale studies because you can't generalize from 
one person's experience. 
But when that one person is you, your n=1 experiment is the most important study in 
the world. 
Every woman in this book Maria, Aisha, Lin, Sarah, Fatima, Keisha, Emma, Maya, 
Amara was running her own n=1 experiment. 
They tracked. They tested. They tried interventions. They paid attention to what 
worked and what didn't. 
They became experts on their own bodies. 
And collectively, those n=1 experiments created a body of knowledge that no medical 
study has replicated. 
Your n=1 experiment matters. 
What you learn about your body, what works for you, what doesn't that's data. That's 
valuable. That's worth documenting and sharing. 
Because the next woman struggling with what you struggled with needs to know she's 
not alone. She needs to know what you tried. She needs to know it's possible. 
 
The Protocol Evolves as You Evolve 
When I started the Foundation Protocol three years ago, I was in severe 
perimenopause. My progesterone was undetectable. My cortisol was dysregulated. I 
was in bio-chaos. 

===== PDF PAGE 235 =====
217 | P a g e  
 
 
The protocol I needed then strict, structured, comprehensive isn't the protocol I need 
now. 
Now I'm in bio-coherence. My hormones are balanced. My sleep is solid. My energy is 
stable. 
I don't track as religiously. I don't need as many supplements. I can be more flexible 
with nutrition and exercise. 
The protocol served its purpose: it stabilized my foundation. 
Now I'm in maintenance mode. 
And in a few years, when I transition into menopause, I'll need a different protocol 
again. 
This is as it should be. Your body isn't static. Your protocol shouldn't be either. 
Adapt. Adjust. Listen. Respond. 
The protocol is a tool, not a prison.

Appendix A: The 90-Day Quick-Start Protocol 
WEEK 1-4: Foundation Phase 
Week 1: Morning Light 
• Within 30 min of waking: 10-30 min outside (or 10,000 lux light therapy lamp) 
• No sunglasses, no windows 
• Track: energy levels, sleep quality (1-10 scale daily) 
Week 2: Add Protein Breakfast 
• 25-30g protein within 1 hour of waking 
• Before coffee, before phone 
• Track: hunger levels, cravings, energy stability 
Week 3: Add Stress Circuit Breakers 
• Morning: 5 min box breathing after breakfast 
• Midday: 5 min phone-free walk 
• Evening: 5 min gentle stretching 
• Track: stress levels, emotional regulation 
Week 4: Add Evening Light Curfew 
• 2 hours before bed: dim lights, blue-light blocking glasses, warm tones 
• No bright overhead lights, no screens (or with blue blockers) 
• Track: sleep onset time, sleep quality, night wakings 
Expected Results After 4 Weeks: 
• 30-50% improvement in sleep quality 
• 25-40% reduction in anxiety/irritability 
• Noticeable improvement in energy and mental clarity 

===== PDF PAGE 242 =====
224 | P a g e  
 
 
• Reduction in cravings and blood sugar crashes 
 
WEEK 5-8: Hormonal Support Phase 
Continue all Foundation practices, add: 
Cycle Tracking (if menstruating): 
• Track cycle day, flow, symptoms 
• Note energy and mood patterns across phases 
• Identify your follicular, ovulatory, luteal, menstrual phases 
Nutrition Adjustments: 
• Follicular phase (Days 1-14): Higher carbs, lighter foods 
• Luteal phase (Days 15-28): More protein/fat, complex carbs 
• Throughout: 7-9 servings vegetables daily, omega-3 rich fish 3x/week 
Supplements (start one at a time, 3-5 days apart): 
• Magnesium glycinate: 400mg before bed 
• Vitamin D3: 2000-5000 IU (test levels first) 
• Omega-3: 2-3g EPA/DHA daily 
• B-complex: 1x daily with food 
Movement: 
• Strength training: 3x/week, 45 min (compound movements) 
• Walking: daily, 30+ min 
• Gentle yoga or stretching: as desired 
• Match intensity to cycle phase 
Expected Results After 8 Weeks: 

===== PDF PAGE 243 =====
225 | P a g e  
 
 
• Cycle more regular (if previously irregular) 
• PMS symptoms reduced 30-50% 
• Energy more stable across month 
• Muscle tone improving 
 
WEEK 9-12: Optimization Phase 
Continue all Foundation and Hormonal practices, add: 
Advanced Tracking: 
• Basal body temperature (if trying to confirm ovulation) 
• Heart rate variability (HRV) via wearable 
• Detailed symptom tracking in journal or app 
Targeted Interventions (based on your specific needs): 
For Insulin Resistance: 
• Berberine: 500mg 3x daily with meals 
• 10-minute walk after each meal 
• Strength training minimum 3x/week 
For Low Progesterone/High Estrogen: 
• Vitamin B6: 50mg daily 
• Magnesium: 400-600mg daily 
• Consider DIM or calcium-d-glucarate (consult provider) 
For Thyroid Support: 
• Selenium: 200mcg daily 
• Zinc: 30mg daily 

===== PDF PAGE 244 =====
226 | P a g e  
 
 
• Iron if ferritin <50 (test first) 
For HPA Axis Dysfunction: 
• Adaptogens: ashwagandha, rhodiola, or holy basil 
• Phosphatidylserine: 300mg before bed (if high evening cortisol) 
• Licorice root: morning only (if low morning cortisol) 
For Autoimmune Support: 
• Continue gluten-free if sensitive 
• Probiotic: multi-strain, 10+ billion CFU 
• Omega-3: increase to 3-4g daily 
• Vitamin D: optimize to 60-80 ng/mL 
Lab Work (at 90 days): 
• Retest baseline labs to assess progress 
• Adjust protocol based on results 
Expected Results After 12 Weeks: 
• Significant improvement in primary symptoms (60-80%) 
• Lab markers moving toward optimal ranges 
• Sustainable habits established 
• Understanding of your unique needs 
 
ONGOING: Maintenance & Fine-Tuning 
Daily non-negotiables: 
• Morning light 
• Protein-first breakfast 

===== PDF PAGE 245 =====
227 | P a g e  
 
 
• Stress circuit breakers 
• Evening wind-down routine 
• 7.5-8.5 hours sleep 
Weekly: 
• Strength training 3x 
• Review tracking data 
• Meal planning 
• Community connection 
Monthly: 
• Review cycle patterns 
• Adjust supplements if needed 
• Schedule self-care 
• Reflect on progress 
Quarterly: 
• Comprehensive lab work 
• Consultation with functional medicine provider 
• Protocol adjustments 
• Reassess goals

BREAKFAST (25-30g protein): 
Template 1: 3 eggs scrambled + 1 cup vegetables + 1/4 avocado

nuts) 
Template 3: Protein smoothie (1 scoop powder + 1 cup milk + 1/2 banana + spinach 
+ nut butter) 
Template 4: Leftover dinner protein (4 oz) + vegetables

Template 1: Large salad (mixed greens + 4 oz protein + vegetables + olive oil dressing 
+ optional grain) 
Template 2: Soup (bone broth-based + protein + vegetables) + side salad 
Template 3: Bowl (protein + roasted vegetables + grain/starch + healthy fat + sauce) 
 
DINNER (25-30g protein): 
Template 1: Protein (4-6 oz) + 2 cups vegetables (roasted or saute ed) + starch (sweet 
potato, rice, quinoa) 
Template 2: One-pot meal (protein + vegetables + starch cooked together) 
Template 3: Lettuce wraps or collard wraps (protein + vegetables + sauce)

• Hard-boiled eggs (2) 
• Greek yogurt (1 cup)

Exercise Progressions 
BEGINNER (Weeks 1-4): 
Strength Training: 
• Bodyweight squats: 3 sets x 10 reps 
• Wall push-ups: 3 sets x 10 reps 
• Assisted lunges: 3 sets x 8 reps each leg 
• Plank hold: 3 sets x 20 seconds 
• Glute bridges: 3 sets x 12 reps 
Cardio: 
• Walking: 20-30 min daily at comfortable pace 
 
INTERMEDIATE (Weeks 5-8): 
Strength Training: 
• Goblet squats (light weight): 3 sets x 12 reps 
• Knee push-ups: 3 sets x 12 reps 
• Walking lunges (bodyweight): 3 sets x 10 each leg 
• Plank: 3 sets x 30-45 seconds 
• Dumbbell rows: 3 sets x 12 reps each arm 

===== PDF PAGE 250 =====
232 | P a g e  
 
 
Cardio: 
• Walking: 30-40 min, add some hills or intervals 
 
ADVANCED (Weeks 9-12+): 
Strength Training: 
• Barbell squats or weighted goblet squats: 3 sets x 10 reps 
• Standard push-ups or dumbbell chest press: 3 sets x 10-12 reps 
• Weighted lunges or Bulgarian split squats: 3 sets x 10 each leg 
• Deadlifts (kettlebell or barbell): 3 sets x 10 reps 
• Overhead press: 3 sets x 10 reps 
• Rows: 3 sets x 10 reps 
Cardio: 
• Walking/jogging: 30-45 min, or HIIT 2x/week (if recovered well)

Appendix B: Biomarker Reference Guide 
WHAT TESTS TO REQUEST 
Essential Baseline Panel (everyone): 
Thyroid: 
• TSH (optimal: 1-2 mIU/L, not just <4.5) 
• Free T3 (optimal: upper half of range) 
• Free T4 (optimal: mid-range) 
• Reverse T3 (optimal: <15 ng/dL) 
• TPO antibodies (optimal: <35 IU/mL) 
• TG antibodies (optimal: <20 IU/mL) 
Metabolic: 
• Fasting glucose (optimal: 75-85 mg/dL) 
• Fasting insulin (optimal: <6 mIU/L) 
• HbA1c (optimal: <5.4%) 
• Lipid panel (cholesterol, HDL, LDL, triglycerides) 
Inflammation: 
• hsCRP (optimal: <1.0 mg/L) 
• Homocysteine (optimal: <7 μmol/L) 
Nutrients: 
• Vitamin D (optimal: 50-80 ng/mL) 
• Vitamin B12 (optimal: >500 pg/mL) 
• Ferritin (optimal: 50-100 ng/mL for women) 
• Magnesium RBC (optimal: >5.0 mg/dL) 

===== PDF PAGE 252 =====
234 | P a g e  
 
 
 
Hormonal Panel (if symptoms present): 
For Menstruating Women: 
Day 3 of cycle: 
• Estradiol (E2) 
• FSH 
• LH 
• Testosterone (total and free) 
Day 21 of cycle (or 7 days before expected period): 
• Progesterone 
Anytime: 
• DHEA-S 
• Sex Hormone Binding Globulin (SHBG) 
For Perimenopausal/Menopausal Women: 
• All of above (can be done any time if cycles irregular/absent) 
• May need multiple tests over time to track patterns 
 
 
 
Adrenal Function: 
Four-point cortisol test (saliva, at home): 
• 8 AM 
• 12 PM 

===== PDF PAGE 253 =====
235 | P a g e  
 
 
• 4 PM 
• 10 PM 
Optimal pattern: 
• Morning highest (15-25 nmol/L) 
• Declining through day 
• Evening lowest (3-8 nmol/L) 
DHEA-S (blood): 
• Optimal varies by age, should be mid-range for your age 
 
Advanced Testing (if indicated): 
For Autoimmune Concerns: 
• ANA (antinuclear antibodies) 
• Celiac panel 
• Specific autoimmune antibodies based on symptoms 
For PCOS: 
• Androgen panel (testosterone, DHEA-S, androstenedione) 
• LH:FSH ratio (>2:1 suggests PCOS) 
• Fasting insulin and glucose (assess insulin resistance) 
For Gut Health: 
• Comprehensive stool test 
• SIBO breath test (if indicated) 
• Food sensitivity panel (IgG, IgA) 
For Genetic Insights: 

===== PDF PAGE 254 =====
236 | P a g e  
 
 
• MTHFR variants 
• Other metabolic variants (if relevant family history) 

===== PDF PAGE 255 =====
237 | P a g e  
 
 
HOW TO READ RESULTS 
Key Principle: "Normal" vs. "Optimal" 
Lab reference ranges are based on 95% of the tested population which includes sick 
people. "Normal" doesn't mean healthy. 
Example: Thyroid 
• Lab says TSH <4.5 is "normal" 
• Functional optimal is 1-2 
• TSH of 3.5 is "normal" but suboptimal (may have symptoms) 
 
OPTIMAL RANGES (not just reference ranges): 
Thyroid: 
• TSH: 1-2 mIU/L 
• Free T3: upper half of range 
• Free T4: mid-range 
• T3:T4 ratio: should be roughly 1:3 or 1:4 
• Antibodies: as close to zero as possible 
Metabolic: 
• Fasting glucose: 75-85 mg/dL 
• Fasting insulin: <6 mIU/L 
• HbA1c: <5.4% 
• Triglycerides: <100 mg/dL 
• HDL: >60 mg/dL for women 
• LDL particle size: large and fluffy (if tested) 
Inflammation: 

===== PDF PAGE 256 =====
238 | P a g e  
 
 
• hsCRP: <1.0 mg/L (ideally <0.5) 
• Homocysteine: <7 μmol/L 
Hormones (reproductive age): 
• Estradiol (Day 3): 30-100 pg/mL 
• Progesterone (Day 21): >10 ng/mL (ideally 15-25) 
• Testosterone: mid-range for your age 
• DHEA-S: mid-range for your age 
Nutrients: 
• Vitamin D: 50-80 ng/mL 
• B12: >500 pg/mL 
• Ferritin: 50-100 ng/mL (women) 
• Magnesium RBC: >5.0 mg/dL 
 
RED FLAGS (get help immediately): 
• TSH >10 or <0.1 
• Fasting glucose >100 
• Fasting insulin >12 
• HbA1c >5.7 
• hsCRP >3.0 
• Ferritin <20 or >200 
• Vitamin D <20 
• Any result your doctor calls "critical" 
 

===== PDF PAGE 257 =====
239 | P a g e  
 
 
WHEN TO RETEST: 
Baseline: Establish your normal 
3 months: After starting new protocol/supplements 
6 months: Mid-protocol check 
Annually: Maintenance monitoring 
As needed: If symptoms change or worsen

Appendix D: Glossary of Terms 
Adaptogens: Herbs that help the body adapt to stress and regulate cortisol (e.g., 
ashwagandha, rhodiola) 
Amenorrhea: Absence of menstruation 
Bioidentical hormones: Hormones that are chemically identical to those the body 
produces naturally 
Bio-coherence: State where all five core systems (hormonal, metabolic, nervous, 
immune, circadian) are functioning in harmony 
Bio-chaos: State where one or more core systems are dysregulated, creating 
cascading dysfunction 
Cycle syncing: Aligning nutrition, exercise, and lifestyle with hormonal phases of the 
menstrual cycle 
Dysbiosis: Imbalance in gut microbiome (too many harmful bacteria, not enough 
beneficial) 
Dysmenorrhea: Painful periods 
Dyspareunia: Painful intercourse 
EDCs (Endocrine-Disrupting Chemicals): Environmental toxins that interfere with 
hormone function (e.g., BPA, phthalates) 
Estrogen dominance: Too much estrogen relative to progesterone 
Follicular phase: First half of menstrual cycle (Days 1-14), characterized by rising 
estrogen 
HPA axis: Hypothalamic-Pituitary-Adrenal axis, the body's central stress response 
system 
HPO axis: Hypothalamic-Pituitary-Ovarian axis, regulates reproductive hormones 
HRV (Heart Rate Variability): Measure of nervous system resilience; higher is better 

===== PDF PAGE 266 =====
248 | P a g e  
 
 
Hypothalamic amenorrhea: Loss of period due to stress, under-eating, or over-
exercising 
Infradian rhythm: ~28-day hormonal cycle unique to women (overlays circadian 
rhythm) 
Insulin resistance: Cells don't respond well to insulin, leading to high blood sugar 
and eventual type 2 diabetes 
Leaky gut: Increased intestinal permeability allowing undigested food particles and 
toxins into bloodstream 
Luteal phase: Second half of menstrual cycle (Days 15-28), characterized by rising 
progesterone 
Medical gaslighting: When healthcare providers dismiss or minimize patients' 
symptoms, especially common for women 
Menarche: First menstrual period 
Metabolic flexibility: Ability to efficiently burn both fat and glucose for fuel 
Molecular mimicry: When food proteins resemble human tissue proteins, 
potentially triggering autoimmune reactions 
Ovulatory phase: Days 14-16 of cycle, when estrogen peaks and ovulation occurs 
PCOS (Polycystic Ovary Syndrome): Hormonal disorder involving insulin 
resistance, elevated androgens, and often irregular cycles 
Perimenopause: Transition period before menopause, typically lasting 4-10 years 
PMDD (Premenstrual Dysphoric Disorder): Severe form of PMS with significant 
mood symptoms 
PMS (Premenstrual Syndrome): Physical and emotional symptoms in luteal phase; 
mild is normal, severe indicates hormonal imbalance 
Polyphenols: Plant compounds that support beneficial gut bacteria and reduce 
inflammation 

===== PDF PAGE 267 =====
249 | P a g e  
 
 
Polyvagal theory: Model describing three states of the nervous system (social 
engagement, mobilization, immobilization) 
Progesterone: Calming hormone produced after ovulation; supports sleep, mood, 
pregnancy 
Somatic: Relating to the body (somatic therapy works with body-based trauma 
release) 
Subclinical hypothyroidism: Thyroid function that's declining but hasn't crossed 
diagnostic threshold (TSH 2.5-4.5) 
Sympathetic nervous system: "Fight or flight" stress response 
Testosterone: Hormone important for muscle, libido, energy (yes, women need it 
too) 
Titration: Gradual exposure to triggers in controlled doses to build tolerance 
Vagal tone: Strength of vagus nerve activation; higher tone = better stress resilience 
Vagus nerve: Nerve connecting brain to organs; key player in parasympathetic ("rest 
and digest") nervous system 

===== PDF PAGE 268 =====
250 | P a g e

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 
To the women who shared their stories for this book; Maria, Aisha, Lin, Sarah, Fatima, 
Keisha, Emma, Maya, and so many others whose names didn't make it into these pages 
but whose experiences shaped every chapter: Thank you for your courage, your 
vulnerability, and your trust. Your stories will help millions of women who come after 
you. You turned your suffering into data, your data into advocacy, and your advocacy 
into hope for others. 
To the 247 researchers, clinicians, data scientists, economists, and policy analysts who 
comprise the Global Women's Bureau team across 32 countries: This book represents 
tens of thousands of hours of collective work; reading studies medicine never 
published, asking questions science never thought to ask, filling data gaps that should 
never have existed. You chose to study what matters rather than what's funded. You 
prioritized impact over prestige. You believed women when institutions did not. This 
book is the synthesis of your dedication. 
To our pilot program participants in Rwanda, Iceland, Portugal, the Netherlands, New 
Zealand, South Korea, Canada, Scotland, Australia, Uruguay, Costa Rica, and Finland: 
You trusted unproven protocols, tracked diligently, reported honestly, and helped us 
refine frameworks that now benefit millions. You were citizen scientists in the truest 
sense. Your willingness to experiment with your own health, to document your 
experiences meticulously, and to share your data openly made evidence-based 
protocols possible. 
To the indigenous healers, traditional medicine practitioners, and ancestral 
knowledge keepers across six continents who shared wisdom with our research 
teams: You preserved understanding of the body as ecosystem, as rhythm, as partner 
truths that Western science is only now beginning to validate. You taught us that some 
knowledge cannot be found in laboratories, and that listening is as important as 
measuring. We are grateful for your patience, your generosity, and your willingness to 
bridge worlds. 
To the doctors, nurses, naturopaths, functional medicine practitioners, and healthcare 
providers around the world who believe women, who spend time listening, who treat 

===== PDF PAGE 299 =====
281 | P a g e  
 
 
root causes instead of just managing symptoms: You are changing medicine from the 
inside. Every patient you truly hear, every dismissal you refuse to participate in, every 
root cause you investigate these acts of care are revolutionary. Keep going. We see you. 
The women you serve see you. 
To the policy makers, healthcare ministers, and government officials in the twelve 
countries implementing the Global Women's Health Mandate: Thank you for having 
the courage to acknowledge that the current system fails women, and the political will 
to change it. Your leadership is creating the blueprint for global transformation. 
Special recognition to the ministries of health in Rwanda, Iceland, and Portugal for 
your early adoption and rigorous implementation. 
To the patient advocacy organizations, women's health nonprofits, and grassroots 
movements who have been fighting for recognition, funding, and reform long before 
we existed: This work stands on your shoulders. Your decades of advocacy, 
consciousness-raising, and political organizing created the conditions that made our 
research possible. We are collaborators in a movement you started. 
To the next generation; the daughters, nieces, students, and young women who will 
inherit the system we're building: You will never know the suffering previous 
generations endured because we're changing the world for you. That's the point. You 
will track your cycles from menarche. You will understand your hormones as a vital 
sign. You will be believed the first time you report symptoms. You will have access to 
comprehensive, evidence-based care. This is our promise to you. 
To the mothers, grandmothers, and great-grandmothers who came before who 
suffered in silence, who were institutionalized for "hysteria," who died of heart attacks 
misdiagnosed as anxiety, who lost years to undiagnosed autoimmune disease, who 
were told their pain was normal: We remember you. We honor you. We're finishing 
what you started when you whispered your symptoms to each other, when you 
preserved folk remedies, when you refused to believe you were crazy even when 
everyone else did. This book is your vindication. 
To every woman reading this who has been dismissed, doubted, or gaslit by the 
medical system: We believe you. Your pain is real. Your symptoms are valid. You 

===== PDF PAGE 300 =====
282 | P a g e  
 
 
deserve better. And this book is our attempt to give you the tools to demand it. You are 
not alone in this fight. You never were. 
To the funders, philanthropic organizations, and institutional partners who chose to 
invest in women's health research when it wasn't fashionable, profitable, or politically 
expedient: Your early support made this work possible. Thank you for betting on 
women before the data proved you should. Your vision enabled research that 
traditional funding mechanisms would never support. 
To the peer reviewers, medical editors, and scientific advisors who scrutinized every 
claim, challenged every assumption, and held us to the highest standards of evidence: 
Your rigor made this book credible. Your willingness to question orthodoxy made it 
revolutionary. Thank you for your intellectual honesty and your commitment to truth 
over convention. 
And finally, to the human body female physiology in all its complexity, variability, and 
brilliance: We're sorry medicine took so long to study you properly. We're sorry 
generations of women were told you were too complicated to understand, too variable 
to research, too difficult to treat. We know better now. You are not a deviation from 
the male norm. You are not a mystery to be solved. You are a sophisticated system that 
deserves to be understood on your own terms. 
We're listening now. 
And we won't stop until every woman on Earth has access to the care, knowledge, and 
dignity she deserves. 
 
With gratitude and resolve, 
The Global Women's Bureau Research Team

AUTHOR’S BIO 
The Global Women's Bureau is a borderless, international research collective founded 
in 2025 with a singular mission: to fill the data gaps that have left women's health in 
the dark ages. 
With team members across more than 32 countries spanning six continents, the 
Bureau represents the most comprehensive, globally-diverse approach to women's 
health research ever assembled. Our researchers, clinicians, data scientists, and policy 
analysts come from Rwanda to Iceland, from South Korea to Costa Rica, from Australia 
to the Netherlands, from Canada to Nigeria, all reflecting the universal reality that 
women's health has been systematically ignored regardless of geography, culture, or 
economic status. 
Our team includes: 
• Endocrinologists and gynaecologists who've witnessed decades of dismissal 
• Immunologists studying why 80% of autoimmune disease affects women 
• Neuroscientists mapping the gender differences in stress response 
• Nutritional scientists challenging one-size-fits-all dietary advice 
• Health economists calculating the $500 billion annual cost of ignoring 
women's health 
• Policy analysts working with 12 governments to implement systemic change 
• Data scientists analyzing patterns across 50,000+ women's health journeys 
 
What makes the Bureau unique: 
We are not Western researchers studying "other" populations. We are a truly global 
collective where a Rwandan community health worker's observations carry equal 
weight to a Swedish endocrinologist's lab data. Where traditional healers from 
indigenous communities collaborate with molecular biologists. Where the lived 

===== PDF PAGE 302 =====
284 | P a g e  
 
 
experience of a 42-year-old woman in Uruguay informs the research questions asked 
by scientists in South Korea. 
This diversity isn't just inclusive, it's scientifically essential. Women's health has 
suffered not only from male-default research, but from Western-centric research that 
ignores how culture, environment, diet, and social structures affect female physiology. 
By centering voices from every continent, we've discovered patterns that single-
country research would never reveal. 
Our work has documented: 
• The systemic dismissal of women's symptoms across all healthcare systems 
• The male-default bias in medical research that excludes or misinterprets 
female physiology 
• Diagnostic delays that persist whether you're in Tokyo or Toronto, Lagos or 
London 
• The economic catastrophe of treating women's health as an afterthought 
• What works: protocols that improve outcomes regardless of geography 
Our pilot programs in Rwanda, Iceland, Portugal, the Netherlands, New Zealand, 
South Korea, Canada, Scotland, Australia, Uruguay, Costa Rica, and Finland have 
demonstrated measurable improvements: 
• 52% reduction in diagnostic delays 
• 67% increase in patient satisfaction 
• 23% decrease in long-term healthcare costs 
• Transformative policy changes at national levels 
Bio-Harmonization: The Bureau Protocol is the culmination of this unprecedented 
global collaboration—a synthesis of rigorous research, clinical expertise from diverse 
medical traditions, and the lived experiences of thousands of women across cultures 
who refused to accept "it's all in your head" as a diagnosis. 

===== PDF PAGE 303 =====
285 | P a g e  
 
 
This book represents what becomes possible when women's health is studied by the 
world, for the world. 
We are headquartered nowhere and everywhere. Our research happens in 
laboratories and villages, hospitals and homes, across every time zone. We answer to 
no single government, pharmaceutical company, or academic institution. We answer 
only to the women whose health we exist to serve. 
We are the Global Women's Bureau. 
We are researchers and millions of women standing together. 
And we're just getting started.
